Appendix B
Hybrid Nonparametric Involutive MCMC and its Correctness

In this section, we present the

Hybrid Nonparametric Involutive Markov chain Monte Carlo
(Hybrid NP-iMCMC),
an inference algorithm
that simulates the probabilistic model
specified by a given SPCF program
that may contains both discrete and
continuous samplers.

To start,
we detail the Hybrid NP-iMCMC inference algorithm:
its state space, conditions on the inputs and
steps to generate
the next sample; and
study how the sampler
moves between states of varying dimensions
and
returns new samples of a nonparametric
probabilistic program.
We then give an implementation of Hybrid NP-iMCMC in SPCF
and demonstrate how
the Hybrid NP-iMCMC method extends the MH sampler. Last but not least,
we conclude with a discussion on
the correctness of Hybrid NP-iMCMC.

B.1
State Spaces

A
state
in the
Hybrid NP-iMCMC algorithm
is a pair
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
of equal
dimension

(but not necessarily equal length)

parameter
and
auxiliary
variables.
The parameter variable
\boldsymbol{x}
is used to
store traces and
the auxiliary variable
\boldsymbol{v}
is used to
record randomness.
Both variables are
vectors of
entropies
,
i.e. Real-Boolean pairs.
This section gives the formal definitions of
the entropy, parameter and auxiliary variables
and the state,
in preparation for the discussion of the Hybrid NP-iMCMC
sampler.

B.1.1
Entropy Space

As shown in
Appendix

A
,
the reduction of a SPCF program is determined by
the input trace
\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathbb{T}:=\bigcup_{n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}(\mathbb{R}\cup\mathbb{2})^{n}
,
a record of drawn values in a particular run of the program.
Hence in order
to simulate a probabilistic model described by a SPCF program,
the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler
should generate Markov chains on the trace space.
However traversing through the trace space
is a delicate business because
the positions and numbers of discrete and continuous values in
a trace given by a SPCF program
may vary.
(Consider
if

coin
:

normal

else
:

coin
.)

Instead,
we pair each value
{\boldsymbol{t}}^{i}
in a trace
\boldsymbol{t}

with a random value
t
of the other type
to make a Real-Boolean pair

({\boldsymbol{t}}^{i},t)
(or
(t,{\boldsymbol{t}}^{i})
).
For instance, the trace
[\mathsf{T},-3.1]
can be made into a Real-Boolean vector
[(1.5,\mathsf{T}),(-3.1,\mathsf{T})]

with randomly drawn values
1.5
and
\mathsf{T}
.
Now, the position of discrete and continuous random variables
does not matter and
the number of discrete and continuous random variables are fixed in each vector.

We call a Real-Boolean pair
an
entropy
and define
the
entropy space

{\mathbb{E}}
to be
the product space

\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{2}
of
the Borel measurable space and
the Boolean measurable space,
equipped with the
\sigma
-algebra

\Sigma_{{\mathbb{E}}}:=\sigma\big(\{R\times B\mid R\in\mathcal{B},B\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{2}}\}\big)
,
and the product measure

\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}}:={\mathcal{N}}\times{\mu_{\mathbb{2}}}

where
{\mu_{\mathbb{2}}}:=\mathsf{Bern}(0.5)
.
Note the Radon-Nikodym derivative

\varphi_{{\mathbb{E}}}
of
\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}}

can be defined as

\varphi_{{\mathbb{E}}}(r,a):=\frac{1}{2}\varphi(r)
.
A
n
-length
entropy vector
is then
a vector of
n
entropies, formally
an element in the product measurable space

({{\mathbb{E}}^{n}},\Sigma_{{\mathbb{E}}^{n}})
.
We write
|{\boldsymbol{x}}|
to mean the
length of the entropy vector
\boldsymbol{x}
.

As mentioned earlier, the parameter variable of
a state
is an entropy vector that stores traces.
Hence, it would be useless if
a unique trace cannot be restored
from an entropy vector.
We found that such a recovery is possible if
the trace is in the support of a
tree representable function.

Say we would like to recover the trace
\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}

that is used to form the entropy vector
\boldsymbol{x}

by pairing each value in the trace with a random value
of the other type.
First we realise that
traces can be made
by selecting either the Real or Boolean component
of each pair in a prefix of
\boldsymbol{x}
.
For example,
traces like
[]
,
[\mathsf{T}]
,
[-0.2]
,

[\mathsf{T},2.9]
and
[-0.2,\mathsf{T},\mathsf{F}]
can be made
from the entropy vector
[(-0.2,\mathsf{T}),(2.9,\mathsf{T}),(1.3,\mathsf{F})]
.
We call these traces
instances
of the entropy vector.
Formally, a trace
\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathbb{T}
is an instance of an entropy vector

\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathbb{E}}^{n}
if

|{\boldsymbol{t}}|\leq n
and

{\boldsymbol{t}}^{i}\in\{r,a\mid(r,a)={\boldsymbol{x}}^{i}\}
for all
i=1,\dots,|{\boldsymbol{t}}|
.
We denote the set of all instances of
\boldsymbol{x}
as

\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\subseteq\mathbb{T}
.
Then, the trace
\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}
must be an instance
of
\boldsymbol{x}
.
Moreover, if we can further assume that

\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}
is in the support of a tree representable function,
then
Proposition

B.1

says
we can uniquely identify
\hat{\boldsymbol{t}}
amongst
all instances of
\boldsymbol{x}
.

Proposition B.1
.

There is at most one (unique) trace
that is both
an instance of an entropy vector
and
in the support of a tree representable function.

Finally, we consider differentiability on the
multi-dimensional entropy space.
We say a function
f:{\mathbb{E}}^{k_{1}}\to{\mathbb{E}}^{k_{2}}

is
differentiable almost everywhere
if
for all
\boldsymbol{i}\in\mathbb{2}^{k_{1}}
,
\boldsymbol{j}\in\mathbb{2}^{k_{2}}
,
the partial function

f_{{\boldsymbol{i}}\to{\boldsymbol{j}}}:\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}

where

f_{{\boldsymbol{i}}\to{\boldsymbol{j}}}(\boldsymbol{r})=\boldsymbol{q}\qquad\iff\qquad f(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{i}))=(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{j}))

is differentiable
almost everywhere
on its domain

\mathsf{Dom}(f_{{\boldsymbol{i}}\to{\boldsymbol{j}}}):=\{\boldsymbol{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}\mid\exists\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}\ .\ f(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{i}))=(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{j}))\}
.
The Jacobian
of
f
on

(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{i}))

is given by

{\nabla f_{{\boldsymbol{i}}\to{\boldsymbol{j}}}}(\boldsymbol{r})
,
if it exists.

B.1.2
Parameter Space

A parameter variable
\boldsymbol{x}
of dimension
n

is an entropy vector of length
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)

where
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}:\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
is a strictly monotone map.
For instance,
the parameter variable

\boldsymbol{x}:=[(-0.2,\mathsf{T}),(2.9,\mathsf{T}),(1.3,\mathsf{F})]

is of dimension two if
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n):=n+1
,
and dimension three if
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n):=n
.
We write
\dim(\boldsymbol{x})
to mean the dimension of
\boldsymbol{x}
and

|{\boldsymbol{x}}|
to mean the length of
\boldsymbol{x}
.
Hence,

\dim(\boldsymbol{x})\leq|{\boldsymbol{x}}|
and

\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(\dim(\boldsymbol{x}))=|{\boldsymbol{x}}|
.
We extend the notion of dimension to traces and
say a trace
\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathbb{T}
has dimension

n
if
|{\boldsymbol{t}}|=\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)
.
Importantly, we assume that
every trace in the support of
w

has a dimension (w.r.t.
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}
),
i.e.
\text{Supp}(w)=\bigcup_{n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(n)}}(w)
.

Formally,
the
n
-dimensional parameter space

({{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}},\Sigma_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}})

is the product of
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)
copies of the entropy space
({\mathbb{E}},\Sigma_{{\mathbb{E}}})

and
the base measure
\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}
on
{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}

is the product of
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)
copies of the entropy measure
\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}}

with the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\varphi_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}
.
For ease of reference, we write

({\mathbb{X}},\Sigma_{{\mathbb{X}}},\mu_{{\mathbb{X}}})

for the one-dimensional parameter space.

B.1.3
Auxiliary Space

Similarly, an auxiliary variable
\boldsymbol{v}
of dimension
n

is an entropy vector of length
\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(n)

where
\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}:\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
is a strictly monotone map.
The
n
-dimensional auxiliary space

({{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}},\Sigma_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}})

is the product of
\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(n)
copies of the entropy space
({\mathbb{E}},\Sigma_{{\mathbb{E}}})

and
the base measure
\mu_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}
on
{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}

is the product of
\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(n)
copies of the entropy measure
\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}}

with the Radon-Nikodym derivative
\varphi_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}
.
For ease of reference, we write

({\mathbb{Y}},\Sigma_{{\mathbb{Y}}},\mu_{{\mathbb{Y}}})

for the one-dimensional auxiliary space.

B.1.4
State Space

A
state
is a pair of

equal dimension
but not necessarily equal length

parameter and auxiliary variable.
For instance
with
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n):=n+1
and
\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(n):=n
,
the parameter variable

\boldsymbol{x}:=[(-0.2,\mathsf{T}),(2.9,\mathsf{T}),(1.3,\mathsf{F})]

and the auxiliary variable

\boldsymbol{v}:=[(1.5,\mathsf{T}),(-2.1,\mathsf{F})]

are both of dimension two and

(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
is a two-dimensional state.

Formally, the
state space

\mathbb{S}
is
the list measurable space of
the product of
parameter and auxiliary spaces of equal dimension,
i.e.
\mathbb{S}:=\bigcup_{n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}({{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}})
,
equipped with the
\sigma
-algebra

\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}:=\sigma\{X_{n}\times V_{n}\mid X_{n}\in\Sigma_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}},V_{n}\in\Sigma_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}},n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\}
and
measure

\mu_{\mathbb{S}}(S):=\sum_{n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\int_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}\ {\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\mid(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\in S\})}\ {\mu_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{v}})
.
We write
\mathbb{S}^{(n)}
for the set
consisting of all
n
-dimensional states.

We extend the notion of instances to states and
say a trace
\boldsymbol{t}
is an instance of a state

(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
if it is an instance of
the parameter component
\boldsymbol{x}
.

The distinction between dimension and length
in parameter and auxiliary variables
gives us the necessary pliancy to
discuss techniques for further extension of the
Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler in
Appendix

C
.
Before that,
we present the inputs to the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler.

B.2
Inputs of Hybrid NP-iMCMC Algorithm

Besides the target density function,
the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler, like iMCMC,
introduces randomness via
auxiliary kernels
and
moves around the state space via
involutions

in order to propose the next sample.
We now examine each of these inputs closely.

B.2.1
Target Density Function

Similar to other inference algorithms for
probabilistic programming,
the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler takes
the
weight function

w:\mathbb{T}\to[0,\infty)

as the target density function.
Recall
w(\boldsymbol{t})

gives the weight of a particular run of
the given probabilistic program
indicated by the trace
\boldsymbol{t}
.
By
Proposition

A.6
,
the weight function
w
is tree representable.
For the sampler to work properly,
we also require weight function
w
to
satisfy the following assumptions.

(H1)

w
is
integrable
,
i.e.
\int_{\mathbb{T}}\ {w}\ \textrm{d}{\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}=:Z<\infty

(otherwise, the inference problem is undefined).

(H3)

w
is
almost surely terminating (AST)
,
i.e.
\mu_{\mathbb{T}}(\{\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathbb{T}\mid w(\boldsymbol{t})>0\})=1

(otherwise, the loop in the Hybrid NP-iMCMC algorithm
may not terminate almost surely).

Virtually all useful probabilistic models can be
specified by SPCF programs
with densities satisfying

(H1)
and
(H3)
.
Exceptions are models that are not normalizable
or diverge with non-zero probability.
(See
Section

A.6

for more details.)

B.2.2
Auxiliary kernels

To introduce randomness,
the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler takes, for each
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
,
a probability
auxiliary kernel

K^{({n})}:{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\leadsto{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}

which gives a probability distribution

K^{({n})}(\boldsymbol{x},\cdot)

on
{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}
for each
n
-dimensional
parameter variable
\boldsymbol{x}
.
We assume
each auxiliary kernel
K^{({n})}
has a
probability density function (pdf)

{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({n})}}:{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}\to[0,\infty)

w.r.t.
\mu_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}
.

B.2.3
Involutions

To move around the state space
\mathbb{S}
,
the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler takes, for each
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
,
an endofunction

{\Phi^{(n)}}
on
{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}

that is both involutive and differentiable
almost everywhere.
We require the set
\{{\Phi^{(n)}}\}_{n}
of involutions
to satisfy the
projection commutation property
:

(H5)

For all
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathbb{S}
where
\dim(\boldsymbol{x})=m
,
if
\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(n)}}(w)\cap\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\not=\varnothing
for some
n
,
then for all
k=n,\dots,m
,

\mathsf{take}_{{k}}({\Phi^{(m)}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}))={\Phi^{(k)}}(\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}))

where
\mathsf{take}_{{k}}
is the projection that
given a state
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
,
takes the first
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(k)
coordinates of
\boldsymbol{x}
and
the first
\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(k)
coordinates of
\boldsymbol{v}

and forms a
k
-dimensional state.

The projection commutation property ensures that the order of applying
a projection and an involution
to a state (which has an instance in the support
of the target density function)
does not matter.

B.3
The Hybrid NP-iMCMC Algorithm

After identifying the state space
and the necessary conditions on the inputs
of the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler,
we have enough foundation to describe the
algorithm.

Given a SPCF program
M

with weight function

w
on the trace space
\mathbb{T}
,
the
Hybrid Nonparametric Involutive Markov chain Monte Carlo (Hybrid NP-iMCMC)
algorithm
generates a Markov chain on
\mathbb{T}

as follows.
Given a current sample
\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
of
dimension
k_{0}

(i.e.
|{\boldsymbol{t}_{0}}|=\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(k_{0})
),

1.

(Initialisation Step)
Form a
{k_{0}}
-dimensional parameter variable

\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({k_{0}})}}
by
pairing each value

{\boldsymbol{t}_{0}}^{i}
in

\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
with
a randomly drawn value
t

of the other type to make
a pair

({\boldsymbol{t}_{0}}^{i},t)
or
(t,{\boldsymbol{t}_{0}}^{i})

in the entropy space
{\mathbb{E}}
.
Note that
\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
is the unique instance of

\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
that is in the support of
w
.

2.

(Stochastic Step)
Introduce randomness to the sampler by
drawing a
{k_{0}}
-dimensional value

\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}})}}

from the probability measure

K^{({{k_{0}}})}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\cdot)
.

3.

(Deterministic Step)
Move around the
n
-dimensional state space
{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}
and
compute the new state
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})

by applying the involution
{\Phi^{(n)}}

to the
initial state

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})

where
n=\dim{(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})}=\dim{(\boldsymbol{v}_{0})}
.

4.

(Extend Step)
Test whether
any instance
\boldsymbol{t}
of
\boldsymbol{x}
is in
the support of
w
.
If so, proceed to the next step
with
\boldsymbol{t}
as the proposed sample;
otherwise

(i)

Extend the
n
-dimensional initial state to
a state

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u}_{0})

of dimension
n+1

where
\boldsymbol{y}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
are values
drawn randomly
from
\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(n+1)}-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(n)}}}
and

\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}{(n+1)}-\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}{(n)}}}

respectively,

(ii)

Go to
Item

3

with the initial state

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})
replaced by

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
.

5.

(Accept/reject Step)
Accept the proposed sample
\boldsymbol{t}

as the next sample with probability

\displaystyle\min\bigg\{1;\;\frac{w{(\boldsymbol{t})}\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k})}}(\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}))\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{x})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{v})}{w{(\boldsymbol{t}_{0})}\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k_{0}})}}(\mathsf{take}_{{k_{0}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}))\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{0})}\cdot|{\det({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})})}|\bigg\}

(1)

where
n=\dim{(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})}=\dim{(\boldsymbol{v}_{0})}
,

k
is the dimension of
\boldsymbol{t}
and

k_{0}
is the dimension of
{\boldsymbol{t}_{0}}
;
otherwise reject the proposal and repeat
\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
.

Remark B.2
.

The integrable assumption on the target density
(
(H1)
)
ensures the inference problem is well-defined.
The almost surely terminating assumption
on the target density
(
(H3)
)
guarantees that the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler
almost surely terminates.
(See
Section

B.4.1

for a concrete proof.)
The projection commutation property on the involutions
(
(H5)
)
allows us to define the invariant distribution

B.3.1
Movement Between Samples of Varying Dimensions

All MCMC samplers that simulate a nonparametric model
must decide how to move between samples of varying dimensions.
We now discuss how the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler
as given in
Section

B.3

achieves this.

Form initial and new states in the same dimension

Say the current sample
\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
has a dimension of
k_{0}
.

Items

1
,
2
and
3

form a
k_{0}
-dimensional initial state

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})
and a new

k_{0}
-dimensional state
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
.

Move between dimensions

The novelty of Hybrid NP-iMCMC is its ability to generate
a proposed sample
\boldsymbol{t}
in the support of the target density
w

which may not be of same dimension as
\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
.
This is achieved by
Item

4
.

Propose a sample of a lower dimension

Item

4
first checks whether
any instance of the parameter-component
\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({k_{0}})}}

of the new state
(computed in
Item

3
)
is in the support of
w
.
If so, we proceed to
Item

5
with that instance, say
\boldsymbol{t}
,
as the proposed sample.

Say the dimension of
\boldsymbol{t}
is
k
.
Then, we must have
k\leq k_{0}
as
the instance
\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathbb{T}
of a

k_{0}
-dimensional parameter
\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({k_{0}})}}

must have a dimension that is lower than or equals to
k_{0}
.
Hence, the dimension of the proposed sample
\boldsymbol{t}

is lower than or equals to
the current sample
\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
.

Propose a sample of a higher dimension

Otherwise (i.e. none of the instances
of
\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({k_{0}})}}
is in the support of
w
)

Item

4
extends the initial state

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({k_{0}})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}})}}
to

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({k_{0}+1})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}+1})}}
;
and computes a new
(k_{0}+1)
-dimensional state

(\boldsymbol{x}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{v}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u})\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({k_{0}+1})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}+1})}}

(via
Item

3
).
This process of incrementing the dimensions
of both the initial and new states is repeated
until an instance
\boldsymbol{t}
of the new state,
say of dimension
n
,
is in the support of
w
.
At which point, the proposed sample is set to be

\boldsymbol{t}
.

Say the dimension of
\boldsymbol{t}
is
k
.
Then, we must have
k>k_{0}
as

\boldsymbol{t}
is
not
an instance of the

k_{0}
-dimensional parameter
\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({k_{0}})}}

but one of
\boldsymbol{x}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}
.
Hence, the dimension of the proposed sample
\boldsymbol{t}

is higher than
the current sample
\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
.

Accept or reject the proposed sample

Say the proposed sample
\boldsymbol{t}
is of dimension
k
.
With the probability given in
Equation

1
,

Item

5
accepts
\boldsymbol{t}

as the next sample
and Hybrid NP-iMCMC updates
the current sample
\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
of dimension
k_{0}
to
a sample
\boldsymbol{t}
of dimension
k
.
Otherwise, the current sample
\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
is repeated
and the dimension remains unchanged.

B.3.2
Hybrid NP-iMCMC is a Generalisation of NP-iMCMC

Given a target density
w
on

\bigcup_{n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
we can set the entropy space
{\mathbb{E}}

to be
\mathbb{R}
and the index maps

\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}
and
\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}
to be identities.
Then,
the
n
-dimensional parameter space

{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}:=\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the
n
-dimensional auxiliary space

{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}:=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
the state space

\mathbb{S}:=\bigcup_{n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}({{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}})=\bigcup_{n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n})
of the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler
matches with those given in
Section

3.2

for the NP-iMCMC sampler.
An instance
\boldsymbol{t}

is then a prefix

{\boldsymbol{x}}^{{1}..{k}}

of a parameter variable
\boldsymbol{x}
.
Moreover, the assumptions
(H1)
,
(H3)
and
(H5)

on the inputs of Hybrid NP-iMCMC are identical to those
(V1)
,
(V3)
and
(V5)
on the inputs of NP-iMCMC.
Hence the Hybrid NP-iMCMC algorithm
(
Section

B.3
)
is a generalisation of the NP-iMCMC sampler (
Figure

2
).

The

B.4
Correctness

The Hyrbid Nonparametric Involutive
Markov chain Monte Carlo (Hyrbid NP-iMCMC)
algorithm is presented in
Section

B.3

for the simulation of probabilistic
models specified by probabilistic programs.

We justify this by
proving that
the Markov chain generated by iterating the
Hybrid NP-iMCMC algorithm
preserves the target distribution, specified by

\displaystyle{\nu:}\ {\Sigma_{\mathbb{T}}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{[0,\infty)}

\displaystyle{U}

\displaystyle\longmapsto{\frac{1}{Z}\int_{U}\ {w}\ \textrm{d}{\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}}\qquad\text{where }Z:=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\ {w}\ \textrm{d}{\mu_{\mathbb{T}}},

as long as
the target density function
w

(given by the weight function of the
probabilistic program)
is integrable (
(H1)
) and
almost surely terminating (
(H3)
);
with a probability kernel

K^{({n})}:{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\leadsto{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}

and
an endofunction
{\Phi^{(n)}}
on
{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}

that is involutive and
differentiable almost everywhere
for each
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\{{\Phi^{(n)}}\}_{n}
satisfies the projection commutation property
(
(H5)
).

Throughout this chapter, we assume the
assumptions stated above, and prove
the followings.

1.

The Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler
almost surely returns a sample
for the simulation
(
Lemma

B.4
).

2.

The state movement in the Hybrid NP-iMCMC
sampler
preserves a distribution on the states
(
Lemma

B.14
).

3.

The marginalisation of the
state distribution which
the state movement of Hybrid NP-iMCMC preserves
coincides with
the target distribution
(
Lemma

B.17
).

B.4.1
Almost Sure Termination

In
Remark

B.2
,
we asserted that
the almost surely terminating assumption
(
(H3)
)
on the target density
guarantees that
the Hybrid NP-iMCMC algorithm
(
Section

B.3
)
almost surely terminates.
We justify this claim here.

Item

3

in the Hybrid NP-iMCMC algorithm
(
Section

B.3
)
repeats itself if
the sample-component
{\boldsymbol{x}}
of the
new state
{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})}

(computed by
applying the involution
{\Phi^{(n)}}

on the extended initial state

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})
)
does not have an instance
in the support of
{w}
.
This loop halts almost surely if
the measure of

\{(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\in\mathbb{S}\mid(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})={\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\text{ and }\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)=\varnothing\}

tends to zero as the dimension
n
tends to infinity.
Since
{\Phi^{(n)}}
is invertible and

|{\det{\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})}|>0

for all
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
and

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\in\mathbb{S}
,

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{S}}(\{(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\in\mathbb{S}\mid(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})={\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\text{ and }\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)=\varnothing\})

\displaystyle={\Phi^{(n)}}_{*}\mu_{\mathbb{S}}(\{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathbb{S}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)=\varnothing\})

\displaystyle<\mu_{\mathbb{S}}(\{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathbb{S}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)=\varnothing\})

\displaystyle=\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)=\varnothing\}).

Thus
it is enough to show that
the measure of a
n
-dimensional
parameter variable
not having any instances
in the support of
w
tends to zero
as the dimension
n
tends to infinity,
i.e.

\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)=\varnothing\})\to 0\qquad\text{as}\qquad n\to\infty.

We start with the following proposition which
shows that
the chance of a
n
-dimensional
parameter variable
having some instances in the support of
w

is the same as
the chance of
w
terminating
before
n
reduction steps.

Proposition B.3
.

\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)\not=\varnothing\})=\mu_{\mathbb{T}}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\text{Supp}^{{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}}}(w))

for all
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
and
all tree representable function
w
.

Proof.

Let
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
and
w
be a tree representable
function.

For each
i\leq n
,
we unpack the set

\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({i})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap A\not=\varnothing\}

of
i
-dimensional parameter variables
that has an instance in the set

A\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{\Omega}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}}

of traces of length
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)

where
\mathbb{\Omega}:=\mathbb{R}\cup\mathbb{2}
.
Write
\pi:\{1,\dots,{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}}\}\to\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{2}\}
for the measurable space

\pi(1)\times\pi(2)\times\dots\times\pi({\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}})

with a probability measure
\mu_{\pi}:=\mu_{\mathbb{\Omega}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}}}
on
\pi
;

{{\pi}^{-1}}
for the “inverse” measurable space of
\pi
,
i.e.
{{\pi}^{-1}}(j):=\mathbb{\Omega}\setminus\pi(j)
for all
j\leq\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)
; and

S
for the set of all such measurable spaces.
Then,
for any
i
-dimensional parameter variable

\boldsymbol{x}
,
\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap A

if and only if
there is some
\pi\in S
where

\boldsymbol{t}\in A\cap\pi

and

\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathsf{zip}(A\cap\pi,{{\pi}^{-1}})
.
Hence,

\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({i})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap A\not=\varnothing\}

can be written as

\bigcup_{\pi\in S}\mathsf{zip}(A\cap\pi,{{\pi}^{-1}})
.
Moreover

\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({i})}}}(\mathsf{zip}(A\cap\pi,{{\pi}^{-1}}))=\mu_{\pi}(A\cap\pi)\cdot\mu_{{{\pi}^{-1}}}({{\pi}^{-1}})=\mu_{\pi}(A\cap\pi)
.

Consider the case where
A:=\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}(w)
.
Then, we have

\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({i})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}(w)\not=\varnothing\}=\bigcup_{\pi\in S}\mathsf{zip}(\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}(w)\cap\pi,{{\pi}^{-1}}).

We first show that this is a disjoint union,
i.e. for all
\pi\in S
,

\mathsf{zip}(\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}(w)\cap\pi,{{\pi}^{-1}})
are disjoint.
Let

\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathsf{zip}(\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}(w)\cap\pi_{1},{{\pi_{1}}^{-1}})\cap\mathsf{zip}(\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}(w)\cap\pi_{2},{{\pi_{2}}^{-1}})

where
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\in S
.
Then, at least one instance
\boldsymbol{t}_{1}
of
\boldsymbol{x}
is in
\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}(w)\cap\pi_{1}
and
similarly at least one instance
\boldsymbol{t}_{2}
of
\boldsymbol{x}
is in
\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}(w)\cap\pi_{2}
.
By
Proposition

B.1
,
\boldsymbol{t}_{1}=\boldsymbol{t}_{2}

and hence
\pi_{1}=\pi_{2}
.

Since
\mathsf{zip}(\text{Supp}^{{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}}}(w)\cap\pi,{{\pi}^{-1}})
are disjoint for all
\pi\in S
,
we have

\displaystyle\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({i})}}}(\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({i})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}(w)\not=\varnothing\})=\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({i})}}}(\bigcup_{\pi\in S}\mathsf{zip}(\text{Supp}^{{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}}}(w)\cap\pi,{{\pi}^{-1}}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in S}\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({i})}}}(\mathsf{zip}(\text{Supp}^{{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}}}(w)\cap\pi,{{\pi}^{-1}}))=\sum_{\pi\in S}\mu_{\pi}(\text{Supp}^{{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}}}(w)\cap\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in S}\mu_{\mathbb{\Omega}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}}}(\text{Supp}^{{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}}}(w)\cap\pi)=\mu_{\mathbb{\Omega}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}}}(\text{Supp}^{{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}}}(w))=\mu_{\mathbb{T}}(\text{Supp}^{{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}}}(w)).

Finally,

\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)\not=\varnothing\}
is equal to

\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({i})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}(w)\not=\varnothing\}\times{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}

and hence

\displaystyle\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)\not=\varnothing\})

\displaystyle=\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({i})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}(w)\not=\varnothing\}\times{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({i})}}}(\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({i})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}(w)\not=\varnothing\})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{\mathbb{T}}(\text{Supp}^{{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}}}(w))

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathbb{T}}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\text{Supp}^{{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(i)}}}(w))

∎

Proposition

B.3
links the
termination of the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler
with that of the target density function
w
.
Hence by assuming that

w
terminates almost surely
(
(H3)
),
we can deduce that the Hybrid NP-iMCMC algorithm (
Section

B.3
) almost surely terminates.

Lemma B.4

(Almost Sure Termination)
.

Assuming
(H3)
,
the Hybrid NP-iMCMC algorithm (
Section

B.3
)
almost surely terminates.

Proof.

Since
{\Phi^{(n)}}
is invertible for all
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
, and

w
almost surely terminates
(
(H3)
),
i.e.
\lim_{m\to\infty}\mu_{\mathbb{T}}(\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\text{Supp}^{j}(w))=1
,
we deduce from
Proposition

B.3
that

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathbb{S}}(\{(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\in\mathbb{S}\mid(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})={\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\text{ and }\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)=\varnothing\})

\displaystyle<\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)=\varnothing\})

\displaystyle=\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}({{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\setminus\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)\not=\varnothing\})

\displaystyle=1-\mu_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\{\boldsymbol{x}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\mid\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)\not=\varnothing\})

\displaystyle=1-\mu_{\mathbb{T}}(\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(i)}(w))

(
Proposition

B.3
)

\displaystyle\to 1-1=0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty.

(
(H3)
)

So the probability of satisfying the condition
of the loop in
Item

3

of Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler tends to zero as
the dimension
n
tends to infinity,
making the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler (
Section

B.3
)
almost surely terminating.
∎

B.4.2
Invariant State Distribution

After ensuring the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler
(
Section

B.3
)
almost always returns a sample (
Lemma

B.4
),
we identify the distribution on the states
and show that it is invariant against
the movement between states of varying dimensions
in Hybrid NP-iMCMC.

State Distribution

Recall a state is an equal dimension
parameter-auxiliary pair.
We define the
state distribution

\pi
on the
state space
\mathbb{S}:=\bigcup_{n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}({{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}})

to be a distribution with density
\zeta
(with respect to
\mu_{\mathbb{S}}
) given by

\displaystyle\zeta(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}):=\begin{cases}\lx@intercol\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z}\cdot w(\boldsymbol{t})\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k})}}(\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}))\hfil\quad\\
&\qquad\text{if }(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}\text{ and }\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)\text{ has dimension }k\\
{0}&\qquad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where

Z:=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\ {w}\ \textrm{d}{\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}

(which exists by
(H1)
) and

\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}
is the subset of
\mathbb{S}
consisting of all
valid states
.

Remark B.5
.

If there is some trace in

\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)

for a parameter variable
\boldsymbol{x}
,
by
Proposition

B.1

this trace
\boldsymbol{t}
is unique and hence

\boldsymbol{x}
represents a sample of
the target distribution.

We say a
n
-dimensional state

(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
is
valid
if

(i)

\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)\not=\varnothing
, and

(ii)

(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{u})={\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
implies

\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{y})\cap\text{Supp}(w)\not=\varnothing
, and

(iii)

\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\not\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}
for all
k<n
.

Intuitively, valid states are the states which,
when transformed by the involution
{\Phi^{(n)}}
,
the instance of the parameter-component of
which does not
“fall beyond” the support of
w
.

We write
\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{n}:=\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}\cap({{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}})
to denote the the set of all
n
-dimensional valid states.
The following proposition shows that involutions preserve the validity of states.

Proposition B.6
.

Assuming
(H5)
,
the involution
{\Phi^{(n)}}
sends
\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{n}

to
\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{n}
for all
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
.
i.e. If
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{n}
,
then
(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{u})={\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{n}
.

Proof.

Let
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{n}

and
(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{u})={\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
.
We prove
(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{u})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{n}

by induction on
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
.

•

Let
n=1
.
As
{\Phi^{(1)}}
is involutive and

(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
is a valid state,

(i)

\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{y})\cap\text{Supp}(w)\not=\varnothing
and

(ii)

(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})={\Phi^{(1)}}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{u})
and

\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)\not=\varnothing
.

(iii)

holds trivially

and hence
(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{u})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{1}
.

•

Assume for all
m<n
,

(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{m}

implies

(\boldsymbol{z}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime})={\Phi^{(m)}}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{m}
.
Similar to the base case, (i) and (ii)
hold
as
{\Phi^{(n)}}
is involutive and

(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
is a valid state.
Assume for contradiction that (iii)
does not hold, i.e. there is
k<n
where

\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{u})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}
.
As
\mathsf{instance}(\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{y}))\cap\text{Supp}(w)\not=\varnothing
,
by
(H5)

and the inductive hypothesis,

\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})=\mathsf{take}_{{k}}({\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{u}))={\Phi^{(k)}}(\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{u}))\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}

which contradicts with the fact that

(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
is a valid state.

∎

We can partition the set

\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}
of valid states.
Let
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
be a
m
-dimensional
valid state.
The parameter variable
\boldsymbol{x}
can be
written as

\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{2})\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}

where

\boldsymbol{t}_{1}\in\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)

is of dimension
k_{0}
,

\boldsymbol{t}_{2}
is a trace where

\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{2})=\mathsf{take}_{{k_{0}}}(\boldsymbol{x})
, and

\boldsymbol{y}:=\mathsf{drop}_{{k_{0}}}(\boldsymbol{x})

where

\mathsf{drop}_{{k}}
drops the first
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(k)
components of the input parameter.
Similarly, the auxiliary variable
\boldsymbol{v}

can be written as
\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{v}_{2}

where

\boldsymbol{v}_{1}:=\mathsf{take}_{{k_{0}}}(\boldsymbol{v})
and

\boldsymbol{v}_{2}:=\mathsf{drop}_{{k_{0}}}(\boldsymbol{v})

where

\mathsf{drop}_{{k}}
drops the first
\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(k)
components of the input parameter.
Hence, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}=\bigcup_{k_{0}=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{m=1}^{\infty}\{

\displaystyle(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{2})\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{v}_{2})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{m}\mid

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{t}_{1}\in\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(k_{0})}}(w),\boldsymbol{t}_{2}\in\mathbb{T},\boldsymbol{y}\in{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(m)-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(k_{0})},\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\in{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}})}},\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\in{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(m)-\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(k_{0})}\}

and the state distribution
\pi
on
the measurable set
S\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}
can be written as

\displaystyle\pi(S)

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{k_{0}=1}^{\infty}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(m)-\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(k_{0})}}\int_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}})}}}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(m)-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(k_{0})}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(k_{0})}}(w)}

\displaystyle\quad[(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{2})\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{v}_{2})\in S\cap\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{m}]\cdot\frac{1}{Z}w(\boldsymbol{t}_{1})\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k_{0}})}}(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{2}),\boldsymbol{v}_{1})\

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad{\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}_{1})}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}_{2})}\ {\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(m)-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(k_{0})}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{y})}\ {\mu_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}})}}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{v}_{1})}\ {\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(m)-\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(k_{0})}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{v}_{2})}

We can now show that the state distribution
\pi

is indeed a probability measure and
the set of valid states almost surely covers
all states w.r.t. the state distribution.

Proposition B.7
.

Assuming
(H1)
,

1.

\pi(\mathbb{S})=1
; and

2.

\pi(\mathbb{S}\setminus\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

1.

Consider the set
\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}
with
the partition discussed above.

\displaystyle\pi(\mathbb{S})

\displaystyle=\pi(\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}})

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{k_{0}=1}^{\infty}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(m)-\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(k_{0})}}\int_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}})}}}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(m)-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(k_{0})}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(k_{0})}}(w)}

\displaystyle\quad[(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{2})\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{v}_{2})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{m}]\cdot\frac{1}{Z}w(\boldsymbol{t}_{1})\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k_{0}})}}(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{2}),\boldsymbol{v}_{1})\

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad{\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}_{1})}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}_{2})}\ {\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(m)-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(k_{0})}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{y})}\ {\mu_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}})}}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{v}_{1})}\ {\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(m)-\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(k_{0})}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{v}_{2})}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{k_{0}=1}^{\infty}\int_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}})}}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(k_{0})}}(w)}\frac{1}{Z}w(\boldsymbol{t}_{1})\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k_{0}})}}(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{2}),\boldsymbol{v}_{1})\cdot

\displaystyle\quad\Big(\bigcup_{\ell_{1}=1}^{\infty}\bigcup_{\ell_{2}=1}^{\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\ell_{1}}}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\ell_{2}}}[(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{2})\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{v}_{1}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{v}_{2})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}]\ {\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\ell_{2}}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{y})}\ {\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\ell_{1}}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{v}_{2})}\Big)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad{\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}_{1})}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}_{2})}\ {\mu_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}})}}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{v}_{1})}\

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{k_{0}=1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(k_{0})}}(w)}\frac{1}{Z}w(\boldsymbol{t}_{1})\cdot\Big(\int_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}})}}}{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k_{0}})}}(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t}_{1},\boldsymbol{t}_{2}),\boldsymbol{v}_{1})\ {\mu_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}})}}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{v}_{1})}\ \Big)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad{\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}_{1})}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}_{2})}\

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\sum_{k_{0}=1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\int_{\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(k_{0})}}(w)}\frac{1}{Z}w(\boldsymbol{t}_{1})\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}_{1})}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}_{2})}\

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\int_{\text{Supp}(w)}\frac{1}{Z}w(\boldsymbol{t}_{1})\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}_{1})}\ =1

2.

Since
\pi
is a probability distribution
and
\pi(\mathbb{S}\setminus\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}})=0
,
the series

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\pi(\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{n})

which equals
\pi(\bigcup_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{n})=\pi(\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}})=1

must converge.
Hence

\pi(\mathbb{S}\setminus{\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}})=\pi(\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}\setminus{\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}})=\sum_{i=n+1}^{\infty}\pi(\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{i})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

∎

Consider the program
eNPiMCMC

in
.
It is similar to

NPiMCMC
(
)
syntactically except
it takes and returns states instead of traces, and
has two additional lines (Lines 2 and 13).
Hence, it is easy to deduce from
Lemma

B.4

that
eNPiMCMC
almost surely terminates.

In
eNPiMCMC
, we group the commands differently
and into two groups:

Line 2-12

An initial valid state
(
x0
,
v0
)

is constructed so that
x0
and
x
*

have the same instance in the support of
w
.

Line 14-22

A proposed state
(
x
,

)

is computed and accepted/rejected.

Invariant Distribution

Take a SPCF program

M
of type
List
(
X
*
Y
)

->

List
(
X
*
Y
)

where the SPCF types
X
and
Y

implements the parameter space
{\mathbb{X}}
and
auxiliary space
{\mathbb{Y}}
respectively.
We define the
transition kernel
of

M
to be the kernel

{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{M}}}}}}}}:\mathbb{S}\leadsto\mathbb{S}
where

{{{{\displaystyle T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{M}}}}}}}}({\boldsymbol{s}},S):=\int_{{{\mathsf{value}_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{M}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{s}}})}}}}}}^{-1}}(S^{\prime})}\ {\mathsf{weight}_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{M}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{s}}})}}}}}}\ \textrm{d}{\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}=\langle\!\langle{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{M}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{s}}})}}}}}\rangle\!\rangle(S^{\prime})

where
s
implements the state
\boldsymbol{s}
and

S^{\prime}
is the set consisting of SPCF terms that
implements states in
S
.
Intuitively,

{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{M}}}}}}}}({\boldsymbol{s}},S)

gives the probability that
the term
M
returns a state
in
S
given the current state
\boldsymbol{s}
.

Proposition B.8
.

Let
M
be a SPCF term of type

List
(
X
*
Y
)

->

List
(
X
*
Y
)
.
If
M
(
s
)
does not contain any scoring
subterm and almost surely terminates
for all SPCF terms
s
,
then
its transition kernel
{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{M}}}}}}}}
is probabilistic.

Proof.

Since the term
M
(
s
)

does not contain
\mathsf{score}(\cdot)
and
terminates almost surely,
by
Proposition

A.11

its value measure must be probabilistic.
Hence

{{{{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{M}}}}}}}}(\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{s}}}}}}},\mathbb{S})=\langle\!\langle{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{M}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{s}}})}}}}}\rangle\!\rangle(\mathbb{S}^{\prime})=\langle\!\langle{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{M}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{s}}})}}}}}\rangle\!\rangle(\Lambda^{0}_{v})=1
.
∎

We say a distribution
\mu
on states
\mathbb{S}
is
invariant

w.r.t. a almost surely terminating
SPCF program
M
of
type
List
(
X
*
Y
)

->

List
(
X
*
Y
)

if

\mu
is not altered after applying
M
, formally

{\int_{\mathbb{S}}\ {T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{M}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{s},S)}\ {\mu}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})=\mu(S)
.

We now prove that

eNPiMCMC
preserves the state distribution

\pi
stated in
Section

B.4.2
by
considering the transition kernels
given by the two steps in
eNPiMCMC

given in
Section

B.4.2
:
find a valid state (Lines 2-12) and
accept/reject the computed proposed state (Lines 13-22).

Finding a Valid State

Assuming the initial state
(
x
*,
v
*)
is valid,

eNPiMCMC
(Lines 2-12) aims to construct a
valid
state
(
x0
,
v0
)

where
x
*
and
x0

share the same instance
t0

that is in the support of the density
w
.

To do this, it first finds the instance

t0
of
x
*

which is in the support of
w
(Line 2).
Say the dimension of
t0
is
k0
(Line 3).
It then forms a
k0
-dimensional
state
(
x0
,
v0
)
by
sampling partners
t

for each value in the trace
t0

to form a
k0
-dimensional
parameter variable
x0
(Line 4); and
drawing a
k0
-dimensional
auxiliary variable from

auxkernel
[
k0
](
x0
)
(Line 5).
Say
v
is the auxiliary value
drawn.
Then, the
k0
-dimensional state
can be written as

(
zip
(
t0
,
t
),
v
)
.

Note that the
k0
-dimensional state

(
zip
(
t0
,
t
),
v
)
might

not
be valid.
In which case, it repeatedly appends

zip
(
t0
,
t
)
and
v

with entropies
(
normal
,

coin
)

until the resulting state is valid (Lines 6-12).
Say
y
and
u
are the entropy
vectors drawn
for the parameter and auxiliary variables
respectively.
Then the resulting state can be written as

(
zip
(
t0
,
t
)+
y
,

v
+
u
)
.

The transition kernel of Lines 2-12
can be expressed

\displaystyle T_{1}((\boldsymbol{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{v}^{*}),S)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(n)-\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(k_{0})}}\int_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(k_{0})}}\int_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}})}}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\ [(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t}_{0},\boldsymbol{t})\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{v}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u})\in S\cap\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{n}]\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k_{0}})}}(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t}_{0},\boldsymbol{t}),\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle\quad{\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t})}\ {\mu_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}})}}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{v})}\ {\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(k_{0})}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{y})}\ {\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(n)-\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(k_{0})}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{u})}\

if
(\boldsymbol{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{v}^{*})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}
and

\boldsymbol{t}_{0}\in\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x}^{*})\cap\text{Supp}(w)

has some dimension
k_{0}\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
; and
0
otherwise.

Remark B.9
.

Recall

\mathsf{zip}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2}):=[({\ell_{1}}^{1},{\ell_{2}}^{1}),({\ell_{1}}^{2},{\ell_{2}}^{2}),\dots,({\ell_{1}}^{n},{\ell_{2}}^{n})]\in(L_{1}\times L_{2})^{n}

for any vectors
\ell_{1}\in L_{1}^{n_{1}}
and

\ell_{2}\in L_{2}^{n_{2}}
with

n:=\min\{n_{1},n_{2}\}
.
Here we extend the definition to lists

\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in(L_{1}\cup L_{2})^{n}
such that
either
({\ell_{1}}^{i},{\ell_{2}}^{i})
or
({\ell_{2}}^{i},{\ell_{1}}^{i})
is in
L_{1}\times L_{2}

for all
i=1,\dots,n
.
Then, we write

\mathsf{zip}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
for the list of pairs in
L_{1}\times L_{2}
.

Proposition B.10
.

Assuming
(H3)
,

T_{1}((\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}),\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}})=1

for all
(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}
.

Proof.

Since Lines 2-12 in
eNPiMCMC
can be
described by a closed SPCF term
that
does not contain
\mathsf{score}(\cdot)
and
terminates almost surely.
By
Proposition

B.8
,
its transition kernel is probabilistic.
Moreover, as this term always return
a valid state, we have

T_{1}((\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}),\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}})=T_{1}((\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}),\mathbb{S})=1
.
∎

.

and
(H3)

.

.
