Accept/Reject Proposed State

After constructing a valid state
(
x0
,
v0
)
, say of dimension
n
,

eNPiMCMC
traverses the state space
via
involution
[
n
]
to obtain a proposal state
(
x
,
v
)
(Line 13).
By
Proposition

B.6
,

(
x
,
v
)
must also be a

n
-dimensional valid state.
Say it has an instance
t

of dimension
k
in the support
of
w
,
then (Line 14-22)

(
x
,
v
)
is accepted with
probability

\displaystyle\alpha(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})

\displaystyle:=\min\Big\{1,\displaystyle\frac{w(\boldsymbol{t})\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k})}}(\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}))\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{x})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{v})}{w(\boldsymbol{t}_{0})\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k_{0}})}}(\mathsf{take}_{{k_{0}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}))\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{0})}\cdot|{\det{\big({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\big)}}|\Big\}

\displaystyle=\min\Big\{1,\displaystyle\frac{\zeta(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{x})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{v})}{\zeta(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{0})}\cdot|{\det{\big({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\big)}}|\Big\}.

The transition kernel for Line 13-22 can be expressed as

T_{2}(\boldsymbol{s},S):=\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})\cdot[{\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in S]+(1-\alpha(\boldsymbol{s}))\cdot[\boldsymbol{s}\in S]

if
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{n}

for some
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
;
and
0
otherwise.

To show that the state distribution
\pi

is invariant against
T_{2}
,
we consider a partition of the set of valid states.
Let
S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}
be the set
of
n
-dimensional valid states
where
\boldsymbol{i}

is the list of
boolean values in all

\boldsymbol{s}\in S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}
and

{\Phi^{(n)}}
maps
\boldsymbol{s}

to a (valid) state with boolean values given by
the list
\boldsymbol{j}
.
Note that both lists
\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j}

of booleans must be of length

\tilde{n}:=\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(n)}+\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}{(n)}
.
Formally,

\displaystyle S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}:=\{\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{n}

\displaystyle\mid\boldsymbol{s}=\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{i})\text{ and }{\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}=\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{j})\text{ for some }\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{n}}\}.

Then, the set
\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}
of valid states
can written as

\bigcup\{S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}\mid\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j}\in\mathbb{2}^{\tilde{n}}\text{ and }n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\}.

Proposition B.12
.

Assuming
(H1)
,
(H3)
and
(H5)
,
for
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
,

\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{n}

and
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}={\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{s})
, we have

\alpha(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\cdot\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\cdot|{\det{\big({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}}(\boldsymbol{s})\big)}}|=\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})\cdot\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s})

where

\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w}):=\zeta(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({m})}}}(\boldsymbol{z})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({m})}}}(\boldsymbol{w})

for any
(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w})\in\mathbb{S}_{m}
.

Proof.

Let

\boldsymbol{s}\in S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}
where
there are

\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{n}}
,

\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j}\in\mathbb{2}^{\tilde{n}}

such that

\boldsymbol{s}=\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{i})
and

\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}:={\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{j})
.
Hence,
taking the Jacobian determinant on
both sides of the equation

{\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{j}}{\boldsymbol{i}}}^{({n})}}\circ{\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}}=\mathsf{id}

gives us

|{\det{\big({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\big)}}|=|{\det{\big({\nabla\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{j}}{\boldsymbol{i}}}^{({n})}}(\boldsymbol{q})\big)}}|=\frac{1}{|{\det{\big({\nabla\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}}(\boldsymbol{r})\big)}}|}=\frac{1}{|{\det{({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}}(\boldsymbol{s}))}}|}.

(2)

Moreover we can write
the acceptance ratio
in terms of
\zeta^{\prime}
as

\alpha(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime\prime})=\min\{1,\displaystyle\frac{\zeta^{\prime}({\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime\prime}))}{\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime\prime})}\cdot|{\det{\big({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime\prime})\big)}}|\}\text{ for any }\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{S}_{m}.

Hence
given
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}={\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{s})
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\cdot\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\cdot|{\det{\big({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}}(\boldsymbol{s})\big)}}|

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s})}{\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})}\cdot|{\det{\big({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\big)}}|\cdot\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\cdot|{\det{({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}}(\boldsymbol{s}))}}|&\text{if }\displaystyle\frac{\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s})}{\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})}\cdot|{\det{\big({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\big)}}|<1\\
\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\cdot|{\det{\big({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}}(\boldsymbol{s})\big)}}|&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(
\boldsymbol{s}={\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})
)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}{\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s})}&\text{if }\displaystyle\frac{\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})}{\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s})}\cdot{|{\det{({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}}(\boldsymbol{s}))}}|}>1\\
\displaystyle\frac{\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})}{\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s})}\cdot{|{\det{({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}}(\boldsymbol{s}))}}|}\cdot\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s})&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(By
Equation

2
)

\displaystyle=\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})\cdot\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s})

∎

Proposition B.13
.

Assuming
(H1)
,
(H3)
and
(H5)
,
the state distribution
\pi
is invariant
against Line 13-22 in
eNPiMCMC
.

Proof.

We aim to show:

\int_{\mathbb{S}}\ {T_{2}(\boldsymbol{s},S)}\ {\pi}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})=\pi(S)

for all
S\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}
.

Let
\boldsymbol{s}
be a
n
-dimensional valid state
and
S\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}
.
Then we can write

T_{2}(\boldsymbol{s},S)

as

[\boldsymbol{s}\in S]+[{\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in S]\cdot\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})-[\boldsymbol{s}\in S]\cdot\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})
.
Hence, it is enough to show that
the integral
of the second and third terms
over all valid states are the same,
i.e.

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}}\ {[{\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in S]\cdot\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})}\ {\pi}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}}\ {[\boldsymbol{s}\in S]\cdot\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})}\ {\pi}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})

First we consider
the valid states in

S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}
where

n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
,

\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j}\in\mathbb{2}^{\tilde{n}}

and

\tilde{n}:=\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(n)}+\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}{(n)}
.
These are
n
-dimensional valid states
with boolean values given by
\boldsymbol{i}

and are mapped by
{\Phi^{(n)}}
to valid states
with boolean values given by
\boldsymbol{j}
.
Then we have

{{\mathsf{zip}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{j})}^{-1}}(S_{{\boldsymbol{j}}{\boldsymbol{i}}}^{({n})})=\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}\big({{\mathsf{zip}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{i})}^{-1}}(S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})})\big)

where

\mathsf{zip}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{j}):\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{n}}\to{\mathbb{E}}^{\tilde{n}}

is a measurable function.
Writing
\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w})
for

\zeta(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({m})}}}(\boldsymbol{z})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({m})}}}(\boldsymbol{w})

for any
(\boldsymbol{z},\boldsymbol{w})\in\mathbb{S}_{m}
,
we have

\displaystyle\int_{S_{{\boldsymbol{j}}{\boldsymbol{i}}}^{({n})}}\ {[\boldsymbol{s}\in S]\cdot\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})}\ {\pi}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{{\boldsymbol{j}}{\boldsymbol{i}}}^{({n})}}\ {[\boldsymbol{s}\in S]\cdot\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})\cdot\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s})}\ {\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\tilde{n}}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})

(Definition of
\pi
)

\displaystyle=\int_{{{\mathsf{zip}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{j})}^{-1}}(S_{{\boldsymbol{j}}{\boldsymbol{i}}}^{({n})})}\ {[\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{j})\in S]\cdot\alpha(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{j}))\cdot\zeta^{\prime}(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{r},\boldsymbol{j}))}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{n}}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{r}})

(
\mathsf{zip}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{j})_{*}\mu_{\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{n}}}=\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\tilde{n}}}
on

S_{{\boldsymbol{j}}{\boldsymbol{i}}}^{({n})}
)

\displaystyle=\int_{{{\mathsf{zip}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{i})}^{-1}}(S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})})}\ [\mathsf{zip}(\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}(\boldsymbol{q}),\boldsymbol{j})\in S]\cdot\alpha(\mathsf{zip}(\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}(\boldsymbol{q}),\boldsymbol{j}))\cdot\zeta^{\prime}(\mathsf{zip}(\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}(\boldsymbol{q}),\boldsymbol{j}))\cdot|{\det{{\nabla\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}(\boldsymbol{q})}}}|\ {\mu_{\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{n}}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{q})}

(Change of variable where

\boldsymbol{r}=\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}(\boldsymbol{q})
)

\displaystyle=\int_{{{\mathsf{zip}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{i})}^{-1}}(S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})})}\ {[{\Phi^{(n)}}(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{i}))\in S]\cdot\alpha(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{i}))\cdot\zeta^{\prime}(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{i}))}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{n}}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{q}})

(

Proposition

B.12
as

{\Phi^{(n)}}(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{i}))=\mathsf{zip}(\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}(\boldsymbol{q}),\boldsymbol{j})

for

(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{i}))\in S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}

)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}}\ {[{\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in S]\cdot\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})\cdot\zeta^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s})}\ {\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\tilde{n}}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})

(
\mathsf{zip}(\cdot,\boldsymbol{i})_{*}\mu_{\mathbb{R}^{\tilde{n}}}=\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\tilde{n}}}
on

S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}
)

\displaystyle=\int_{S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}}\ {[{\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in S]\cdot\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})}\ {\pi}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})

Recall the set
\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}

of all valid states
can be written as

\bigcup\{S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}\mid\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j}\in\mathbb{2}^{\tilde{n}}\text{ and }n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}\}
.
Hence, we conclude our proof with

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}}\ {[{\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in S]\cdot\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})}\ {\pi}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j}\in\mathbb{2}^{\tilde{n}}}\int_{S_{{\boldsymbol{i}}{\boldsymbol{j}}}^{({n})}}\ {[{\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in S]\cdot\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})}\ {\pi}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{j}\in\mathbb{2}^{\tilde{n}}}\int_{S_{{\boldsymbol{j}}{\boldsymbol{i}}}^{({n})}}\ {[\boldsymbol{s}\in S]\cdot\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})}\ {\pi}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}}\ {[\boldsymbol{s}\in S]\cdot\alpha(\boldsymbol{s})}\ {\pi}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}}).

∎

Since the transition kernel of

eNPiMCMC
is the composition of

T_{1}
and
T_{2}

and both

T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are
invariant against
\pi

(
Propositions

B.11
and
B.13
),
we deduce that

eNPiMCMC
preserves the state distribution
\pi
.

Lemma B.14

(State Invariant)
.

\pi
is the invariant distribution of the Markov chain generated by iterating
eNPiMCMC
.

B.4.3
Marginalised Markov Chains

As discussed above, the Markov chain

\{\boldsymbol{s}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}

generated by iterating
eNPiMCMC

(which has invariant distribution
\pi

(
Lemma

B.14
))
has elements on the state space
\mathbb{S}

and
not
the trace space
\mathbb{T}
.
The chain we are in fact interested in is
the
marginalised
chain

\{\mathfrak{m}(\boldsymbol{s}_{i})\}_{i\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
where
the measurable function

\mathfrak{m}:\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}\to\mathbb{T}

takes a valid state
\boldsymbol{s}=(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})

and returns the instance of the
parameter variable
\boldsymbol{x}
that is in
the support of the target density function

w
.

In this section we show that
this marginalised chain simulates the
target distribution
\nu
.
Let
{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{NPiMCMC}}}}}}}}:\mathbb{T}\leadsto{\mathbb{T}}
be a kernel
such that

{{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{NPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{t},A):=\begin{cases}T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{eNPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{s},{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A))&\text{if }\boldsymbol{t}\in\text{Supp}(w)\text{ and }\boldsymbol{s}\in{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(\{\boldsymbol{t}\})\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Comparing the commands of

NPiMCMC
and
eNPiMCMC

in
and
,
we claim that
{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{NPiMCMC}}}}}}}}

is
the
transition kernel of

NPiMCMC
.

Proposition B.15
.

We consider some basic properties of

{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{NPiMCMC}}}}}}}}
.

1.

{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{NPiMCMC}}}}}}}}
is well-defined.

2.

{{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{eNPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{s},{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A))=T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{NPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\mathfrak{m}(\boldsymbol{s}),A)

for all
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}
and

A\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{T}}
.

Proof.

1.

Let
\boldsymbol{t}\in\text{Supp}(w)
and

A\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{T}}
.
Say
\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}\in{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(\{\boldsymbol{t}\})
.
Since only the instance of the input
state matters in
eNPiMCMC

(
),
the value of
{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{NPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{t},A)

given by
\boldsymbol{s}
and
\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}

are the same, i.e.
{{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{eNPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{s},{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A))=T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{eNPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime},{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A)).

2.

Let
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}
and

A\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{T}}
. Then,

{{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{NPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\mathfrak{m}(\boldsymbol{s}),A)=T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{eNPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime},{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A))
for some

\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime}\in{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(\{\mathfrak{m}(\boldsymbol{s})\})
.
Since
\boldsymbol{s}\in{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(\{\mathfrak{m}(\boldsymbol{s})\})
,
we have
{{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{eNPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime},{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A))=T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{eNPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{s},{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A))
.

∎

To show
{T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{NPiMCMC}}}}}}}}

preserves the target distribution,
we consider a distribution
\pi_{n}

on each of the
n
-dimensional state space

\mathbb{S}^{(n)}:={{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}
with density

\zeta_{n}
(w.r.t.
\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{(n)}}
)
given by

\zeta_{n}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z_{n}}\cdot w(\boldsymbol{t})\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k})}}(\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}))&\text{if }\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)\text{ has dimension }k\leq n\\
{0}&\qquad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

where
Z_{n}:=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\ {[|{\boldsymbol{t}}|\leq{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)}]\cdot w(\boldsymbol{t})}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{t}})
.
Notice that
Z_{n}\cdot\zeta_{n}
and
Z\cdot\zeta
are the same,
except on non-valid states.
The following proposition shows how the
state distribution
\pi
can be
represented using
\pi_{n}
.

Proposition B.16
.

Let
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
.

1.

\pi_{n}
is a probability measure.

2.

For
k\leq n
,

Z_{k}\cdot\pi_{k}=Z_{n}\cdot{\mathsf{take}_{{k}}}_{*}\pi_{n}

on
\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}
.

3.

Let
g^{(n)}:\mathbb{S}^{(n)}\rightharpoonup{\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}}

be the partial measurable function that
returns the projection of the input state
that is valid, if it exists. Formally,

g^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{s})=\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{s})

if

\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}
.
Then

Z\cdot\pi=Z_{n}\cdot g^{(n)}_{*}\pi_{n}
on
{\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}}
.

Proof.

1.

Consider
\pi_{n}(\mathbb{S}^{(n)})
,

\displaystyle\pi_{n}(\mathbb{S}^{(n)})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ \int_{\mathbb{S}^{(n)}}\ [\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})]\cdot[|{\boldsymbol{t}}|=\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(k)]\cdot\frac{1}{Z_{n}}\cdot w(\boldsymbol{t})\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k})}}(\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}))\ {\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{(n)}}}{(\textrm{d}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}))}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ \int_{\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(k)}(w)}\ \int_{\mathbb{T}}\ \int_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k})}}}\ \frac{1}{Z_{n}}\cdot w(\boldsymbol{t})\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k})}}(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime}),\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}))\ {\mu_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k})}}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime})}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime})}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ \int_{\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(k)}(w)}\ \int_{\mathbb{T}}\ \frac{1}{Z_{n}}\cdot w(\boldsymbol{t})\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime})}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t})}

(
K^{({k})}
is a probability kernel)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\ [|{\boldsymbol{t}}|\leq\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)]\cdot\frac{1}{Z_{n}}\cdot w(\boldsymbol{t})\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t})}=1

2.

Let
S\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}

where
S\subseteq{\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}}
.
Hence

Z_{k}\cdot\rho_{k}(\boldsymbol{s})=Z_{k}\cdot\rho_{k}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})

if
\boldsymbol{s}\in S

and
\boldsymbol{s}=\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})
.
Then,

\displaystyle Z_{n}\cdot({\mathsf{take}_{{k}}}_{*}\pi_{n})(S)

\displaystyle=Z_{n}\cdot\pi_{n}({{{\mathsf{take}_{{k}}}}^{-1}}{(S)})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{(n)}}\ {[\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})\in S]\cdot Z_{n}\cdot\zeta_{n}(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{(n)}}}(\textrm{d}{(\boldsymbol{s}^{\prime})})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{(k)}}\ [(\boldsymbol{s})\in S]\cdot Z_{k}\cdot\zeta_{k}(\boldsymbol{s})\cdot\ {\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{(k)}}}{(\textrm{d}(\boldsymbol{s}))}

\displaystyle=Z_{k}\cdot\pi_{k}(S)

3.

Let
S\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{S}}

where
S\subseteq{\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}}
.
Then,
Z\cdot\rho(\boldsymbol{s})=Z_{k}\cdot\rho_{k}(\boldsymbol{s})

for all
\boldsymbol{s}\in S\cap\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}
.

\displaystyle Z\cdot\pi(S)

\displaystyle=\int_{S}\ {[\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}]\cdot Z\cdot\zeta(\boldsymbol{s})}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{S}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{S}\ {[\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}]\cdot Z\cdot\zeta(\boldsymbol{s})}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{(k)}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{S}\ {[\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}]\cdot Z_{k}\cdot\zeta_{k}(\boldsymbol{s})}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{(k)}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}Z_{k}\cdot\pi_{k}(S\cap\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k})

\displaystyle=Z_{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}{\mathsf{take}_{{k}}}_{*}\pi_{n}(S\cap\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k})

(i)

\displaystyle=Z_{n}\cdot\pi_{n}(\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}\{\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathbb{S}^{(n)}\mid\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{s})\in S\cap\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}\})

\displaystyle=Z_{n}\cdot g^{(n)}_{*}\pi_{n}(S).

∎

Lemma B.17

(Invariant)
.

Assuming
(H1)
,
(H3)
and
(H5)
,

\nu
is the invariant distribution of
the Markov chain generated by
iterating the Hyrbid NP-iMCMC algorithm (
Section

B.3
).

Proof.

Assuming
(1)
\nu=\mathfrak{m}_{*}\pi
on
\mathbb{T}
and
(2)
\mu_{\mathbb{T}}=\mathfrak{m}_{*}\mu_{\mathbb{S}}
on
\text{Supp}(w)
, we have
for any
A\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{T}}
,

\displaystyle\nu(A)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{m}_{*}\pi(A)

(1)

{\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}}\ {T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{eNPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{s},{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A))}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{S}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})

(
Lemma

B.14
)

{\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}}\ {T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{eNPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{s},{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A))}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{S}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})

{\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}}\ {T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{NPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\mathfrak{m}(\boldsymbol{s}),A)}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{S}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{s}})

(
Proposition

B.15
.ii)

{\displaystyle=\int_{\text{Supp}(w)}\ {T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{NPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{t},A)}\ {\mathfrak{m}_{*}\mu_{\mathbb{S}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{t}})

{\displaystyle=\int_{\text{Supp}(w)}\ {T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{NPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{t},A)}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{t}})

(2)

{\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\ {T_{\hbox{\pagecolor{background}\lstinline{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@frame\footnotesize{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_emph4}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{NPiMCMC}}}}}}}}(\boldsymbol{t},A)}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{t}}).

It is enough to show (1) and (2).

1.

Let
A\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{T}}
where

A\subseteq\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)}(w)
and

\delta>0
.
Then partitioning
{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A)
using
\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}
, we have for sufficiently large
m
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{*}\pi(A)

\displaystyle=\pi\left(\bigcup_{k=1}^{m}{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A)\cap\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}\right)+\pi\left(\bigcup_{k=m+1}^{\infty}{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A)\cap\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle<\frac{Z_{m}}{Z}\cdot g^{(m)}_{*}\pi_{m}\left(\bigcup_{k=1}^{m}{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A)\cap\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}\right)+\delta

(
Proposition

B.15
.iii,

Proposition

B.16
.ii)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Z_{m}}{Z}\cdot\pi_{m}(\{(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime})\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{v})\mid\boldsymbol{t}\in A,\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T},\boldsymbol{y}\in{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(m)-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)},\boldsymbol{v}\in{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({m})}}\})+\delta

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z}\int_{A}\ \int_{\mathbb{T}}\ \int_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(m)-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)}}\ w(\boldsymbol{t})\cdot

\displaystyle\qquad\big(\int_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({m})}}}\ {{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({n})}}(\mathsf{take}_{{n}}(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime})\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{v}))}\ {\mu_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({m})}}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{v}})\big)

\displaystyle\quad{\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(m)-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{y})}{\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime})}{\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}{(\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{t})}+\delta

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z}\int_{A}\ {w(\boldsymbol{t})}\ {\mu_{\mathbb{T}}}(\textrm{d}{\boldsymbol{t}})+\delta

(
K^{({n})}
is a probability kernel)

\displaystyle=\nu(A)+\delta.

For any measurable set
A\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{T}}
,
we have

\mathfrak{m}_{*}\pi(A)=\mathfrak{m}_{*}\pi(A\cap\text{Supp}(w))=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathfrak{m}_{*}\pi(A\cap\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)}(w))\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\nu(A\cap\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)}(w))=\nu(A\cap\text{Supp}(w))=\nu(A)
.
Since both
\nu
and
\pi
are
probability distributions, we also have

\nu(A)=1-\nu(\mathbb{T}\setminus A)\leq 1-\mathfrak{m}_{*}\pi(\mathbb{T}\setminus A)=1-(1-\mathfrak{m}_{*}\pi(A))=\mathfrak{m}_{*}\pi(A)
.
Hence

\mathfrak{m}_{*}\pi=\nu
on
\mathbb{T}
.

2.

Similarly, let
A\in\Sigma_{\mathbb{T}}

where
A\subseteq\text{Supp}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)}(w)

and
\delta>0
.
Then by
Proposition

B.15
.iii,
for sufficiently large
m
, we must have

\mu_{\mathbb{S}}(\bigcup_{k=m+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k})=\mu_{\mathbb{S}}(\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}\setminus\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{\leq m})<\delta
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{*}\mu_{\mathbb{S}}(A)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\bigcup_{k=1}^{m}{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A)\cap\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}\right)+\mu_{\mathbb{S}}\left(\bigcup_{k=m+1}^{\infty}{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A)\cap\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle<\sum_{k=1}^{m}\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{(k)}}({{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A)\cap\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k})+\delta

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{(m)}}(\{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathbb{S}^{(m)}\mid\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\in{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A)\cap\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}\})+\delta

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{(m)}}(\bigcup_{k=1}^{m}\{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathbb{S}^{(m)}\mid\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\in{{\mathfrak{m}}^{-1}}(A)\cap\mathbb{S}^{\textrm{valid}}_{k}\})+\delta

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\mathbb{S}^{(m)}}(\{(\mathsf{zip}(\boldsymbol{t},\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime})\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{v})\mid\boldsymbol{t}\in A,\boldsymbol{t}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T},\boldsymbol{y}\in{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(m)-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)},\boldsymbol{v}\in{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({m})}}\})+\delta

\displaystyle=\mu_{\mathbb{T}}(A)+\delta.

Then the proof proceeds as in (1). Note that since
w
almost surely terminating (
(H3)
),

\mathfrak{m}_{*}\mu_{\mathbb{S}}(\text{Supp}(w))=\mu_{\mathbb{T}}(\text{Supp}(w))=1

∎

B.4.4
Correctness of NP-iMCMC

The correctness of the NP-iMCMC sampler (
Figure

2
)
can be deduce from
Lemma

B.17

and the fact that Hyrbid NP-iMCMC is a generalisation of NP-iMCMC

See
3.2

Appendix C
Transforming Nonparametric Involutive MCMC

In this section,
we discuss how the techniques
discussed in
(
Neklyudov et al. 2020
)

can be applied to the Hybrid NP-iMCMC
sampler presented in
Appendix

B
.
Hence
instances of the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler,
such as NP-MH and NP-HMC,
can be extended
using these techniques
to become more flexible and efficient.

We assume the input
target density function
w:\mathbb{T}\to[0,\infty)

is tree representable,
integrable (
(H1)
) and
almost surely terminating (
(H3)
).

C.1
State-dependent Hybrid NP-iMCMC Mixture

Say we want to use multiple Hybrid NP-iMCMC samplers
to simulate the posterior given by
the target density function
w
.
The following technique allows us to ‘mix’
Hybrid NP-iMCMC samplers
in such a way that the resulting sampler still
preserves the posterior.

Given a collection of Hybrid NP-iMCMC samplers,
indexed by
m\in{{\mathbb{E}}^{\alpha}}
,
for some
\alpha\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
,
each with
auxiliary kernels

\{K^{({n})}_{m}:{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\leadsto{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}\}_{n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}
and
involutions

\{{\Phi^{(n)}}_{m}:{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}\to{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}\}_{n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}

satisfying the projection commutation property
(
(H5)
),
the
State-dependent Hybrid NP-iMCMC Mixture
sampler
determines which Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler to use by
drawing an indicator
m\in{{\mathbb{E}}^{\alpha}}

from a probability measure

K_{M}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\cdot)

where
K_{M}:\bigcup_{n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\leadsto{\mathbb{E}}^{m}

is a probability kernel and

\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
is the entropy vector constructed from
the current sample
\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
at the initialisation step
(
Item

1
of Hybrid NP-iMCMC).
Then, using the
m
-indexed Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler,
a proposal
\boldsymbol{t}
is generated and
accepted with a modified probability that
includes the probability of picking
m
,
namely

\displaystyle\min\bigg\{1;\;

\displaystyle\frac{w{(\boldsymbol{t})}\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k})}}_{m}(\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}))\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{x})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{v})}{w{(\boldsymbol{t}_{0})}\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k_{0}})}}_{m}(\mathsf{take}_{{k_{0}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}))\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{0})}\cdot\frac{{\mathsf{pdf}K_{M}}({\boldsymbol{x}_{0}}^{{1}..{k_{0}}},m)}{{\mathsf{pdf}K_{M}}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{{1}..{k}},m)}\cdot|{\det({\nabla{\Phi^{(n)}}_{m}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})})}|\bigg\}

where

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})
is the (possibly extended) initial state,

(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
is the new state,

n=\dim{(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})}=\dim{(\boldsymbol{v}_{0})}
,

k_{0}
is the dimension of
{\boldsymbol{t}_{0}}

(i.e.
|{\boldsymbol{t}_{0}}|=\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(k_{0})}
) and

k
is the dimension of
\boldsymbol{t}

(i.e.
|{\boldsymbol{t}}|=\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(k)}
).

Pseudocode

This sampler can be implemented in SPCF as
the
MixtureNPiMCMC
function in
.
(Terms specific to this technique
are highlighted.)
We assume the following SPCF terms exists:

)

;

of type

(

)*(
R
*
B
)^
l

R

implements
the probability density function

{\mathsf{pdf}K_{M}}:\bigcup_{n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{E}}^{\alpha}}\to\mathbb{R}

; and
for each
m\in{{\mathbb{E}}^{\alpha}}
and
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
,

auxkernel
[
n
][

]

implements the auxiliary kernel
K^{({n})}_{m}
;

][

]

;

.

⬇

(

k0

Correctness

Similar to the correctness arguments in

(
Neklyudov et al. 2020
)
,
we show that
the State-dependent Hybrid NP-iMCMC Mixture sampler
is correct
by formulating
MixtureNPiMCMC

as an instance of
NPiMCMC

(
).
This means specifying

auxkernel
[
n
]

and
involution
[
n
]

in
NPiMCMC

and arguing that the resulting

NPiMCMC
function is equivalent to
MixtureNPiMCMC
.

The SPCF terms
mixauxkernel
[
n
]

and
mixinvolution
[
n
]

given in

should suffice.
The auxiliary space is expanded to embed
the indicator
m
in such a way that
the auxiliary variable
mixv

is in the space
{{\mathbb{E}}^{\alpha}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}

where its first
\ell
-th components
mixv
[:
l
]
gives
m

and the rest
mixv
[
l
:]
gives
v
.
Since the auxiliary space is expanded
to include the indicator,
the maps

mixindexX
and
mixindexY

and the projection
mixproj

are modified accordingly.

To see how the
NPiMCMC
function with

auxkernel
[
n
]
replaced by
mixauxkernel
[
n
]
and

involution
[
n
]
replaced by
mixinvolution
[
n
]

is equivalent to
MixtureNPiMCMC
,
we onyl need to consider the probability density of

mixauxkernel
[
k
]
at

mixproj
((
x
,
mixv
),
k
)
.

⬇

C.2
Direction Hybrid NP-iMCMC Algorithm

Sometimes it is difficult to specify involutions
that explores the model fully.
The following technique tells us that
bijections are good enough.

Given endofunctions

f^{(n)}
on
{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}

that are differentiable almost everywhere and bijective
for each
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}

such that
the sets
\{f^{(n)}\}_{n}
and

\{{{f^{(n)}}^{-1}}\}_{n}

satisfy the projection commutative property
(
(H5)
),
the
Direction Hybrid NP-iMCMC
algorithm
randomly use either
f^{(n)}
or

{{f^{(n)}}^{-1}}
to move around the state space
and proposes a new sample.

Pseudocode

This sampler can be expressed in SPCF as the

DirectionNPiMCMC
function in
.
(Terms specific to this technique are highlighted.)
We assume for each
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
and
d\in\mathbb{2}
,
there is a SPCF term

bijection
[
n
][
d
]
where

bijection
[
n
][
True
]
implements
the bijection
f^{(n)}
and

bijection
[
n
][
False
]
implements
the inverse
{{f^{(n)}}^{-1}}
and
SPCF term

absdetjacbij
[
n
][
d
]
that implements
the absolute value of the Jacobian determinant of

f^{(n)}
if
d

=

True
and
the inverse
{{f^{(n)}}^{-1}}
otherwise.

We show that

DirectionNPiMCMC

can be formulated as an instance of

NPiMCMC
(
)
with a specification of

auxkernel
[
n
]
and
involution
[
n
]
.

The SPCF terms

[

]
and

[

]

would work.
The auxiliary space is expanded to include
the direction variable
d0

so that
the auxiliary variable
dirv0

is in the space
{\mathbb{E}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}

where
the Boolean-component

[0][1]

d0

and
the second to last coordinates

[1:]
gives
v0
.
(Note the value of

[0][0]

and is only used to make

[0]

Since the auxiliary space is expanded,
the maps

and
dirindexY

and the projection

dirproj

are modified accordingly.

To see how the

[

dirauxkernel
[
n
]
and

[
n
]
replaced by

[
n
]

is equivalent to

,
we first consider
the density of

dirauxkernel
[
k0
]

at
dirproj
((
x0
,
dirv0
),
k0

.

⬇

[

C.3
Persistent Hybrid NP-iMCMC Algorithm

It is known that irreversible transition kernels
(those that does not satisfy detailed balance)
have better mixing times,
i.e. converges more quickly to the target distribution,
compared to reversible ones.
The following technique gives us a method to
transform Hybrid NP-iMCMC algorithms to
irreversible ones that still preserves the target distribution.
The key is to compose
the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler with a transition kernel
so that
the resulting algorithm does not satisfy detailed balance.

The
Persistent Hybrid NP-iMCMC
algorithm
is a MCMC algorithm similar to
the Direction Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler
in which the direction variable is used
to determine auxiliary kernels
(
\{K^{({n})}_{1}:{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\leadsto{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}\}_{n}

or

\{K^{({n})}_{2}:{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\leadsto{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}\}_{n}
) and
bijections
(
\{f^{(n)}:{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}\to{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}\}_{n}

or
\{{{f^{(n)}}^{-1}}:{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}\to{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}\}_{n}
)
being used.
The difference is that
Persistent Hybrid NP-iMCMC keeps track of the direction
(instead of sampling a fresh one in each iteration)
and flips it strategically to
make the resulting algorithm irreversible.

Pseudocode

This sampler can be expressed in SPCF as

PersistentNPiMCMC

in
.
(Terms specific to this technique are highlighted.)
In addition to the SPCF terms in
DirectionNPiMCMC
,
we assume there is a SPCF term

auxkernel
[
n
][
d
]
such that

auxkernel
[
n
][
True
]
implements
the auxiliary kernel

K^{({n})}_{1}:{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\leadsto{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}
and

pdfauxkernel
[
n
][
True
]

its pdf
{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({n})}}_{1}

and similarly for

auxkernel
[
n
][
False
]

and

pdfauxkernel
[
n
][
False
]
.
Note that

PersistentNPiMCMC

updates samples
on the space
{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times\mathbb{2}
,
which can easily be marginalised to
{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}

by taking the first
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)
components.

)])

(

(

),

The

[

[
n
]
and

involution
[
n
]
replaced by
perinvolution
[
n
]

is
almost
equivalent to

PersistentNPiMCMC
,
except

NPiMCMC

induces a transition kernel on
{\mathbb{E}}\times{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}

whereas
PersistentNPiMCMC

induces a transition kernel on
\mathbb{2}\times{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}
; and
when the proposal
t
is accepted,

NPiMCMC

returns
d
whereas

PersistentNPiMCMC
returns

not

d
.

These differences can be reconciled by
composing
NPiMCMC

with
flipdir
,
which is an instance of
NPiMCMC

which skips the stochastic step
and has an involution
that flips the direction variable stored
in
perx0
[0][1]
.
The composition generates a Markov chain
on
{\mathbb{E}}\times{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}
and
marginalising it to a Markov chain on

\mathbb{2}\times{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}

gives us the same result as

PersistentNPiMCMC
.

Appendix D
Multiple Step Nonparametric Involutive MCMC

In this section, we study
the
Multiple Step NP-iMCMC
sampler,
a generalisation of the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler (
Section

B.3
)
(and also of NP-iMCMC (
Figure

2
)),
where the involution is applied
multiple times to generate a proposed state.

D.1
Motivation

Item

4
in the
Hyrbid NP-iMCMC sampler may seem inefficient. While it terminates almost surely (thanks to
(H3)
),
This is because
whenever the dimension of the state is changed,
the algorithm has to “re-run” the involution again
(
Item

4
.ii).
This means
the expected number of iterations may be infinite.

To remedy this problem, we introduce two
new concepts:

•

The
slice function
which
might make “re-runs”
(
Item

4
.ii) quicker.

•

The
Multiple Step NP-iMCMC

sampler, a generaliation
of Hyrbid NP-iMCMC,
which uses a list of bijections
to move around the state space.

D.2
Slice function

For each dimension
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
,
we call the measurable function

s^{({n})}:\mathbb{S}^{(n)}\to{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n-1)}\times{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(n)-\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}(n-1)}

a
slice
of
the endofunction
{\Phi^{(n)}}
on
\mathbb{S}^{(n)}
if
it captures the movement of the
n
-th
dimensional states with
an instance of dimension
lower than

n
.
Formally, this means

s^{({n})}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})=(\mathsf{drop}_{{n-1}}\circ{\Phi^{(n)}})(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\quad\text{if }\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x})\cap\text{Supp}(w)\text{ and }|{\boldsymbol{t}}|<\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n).

Note we can always define
a slice of
{\Phi^{(n)}}

by setting

s^{({n})}:=\mathsf{drop}_{{n-1}}\circ{\Phi^{(n)}}
.

With the slice function
s^{({n})}

defined for each involution
{\Phi^{(n)}}
,

Item

4
.ii in the
Hyrbid NP-iMCMC algorithm
(
Section

B.3
):

(Step 4.ii)

Move around the
n+1
-dimensional state
space
{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n+1})}}\times{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n+1})}}
and
compute the new state
by applying the involution
{\Phi^{(n+1)}}

to the initial state

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
;

can be replaced by the following Step 4.ii’:

(Step 4.ii’)

Replace and extend the

n
-dimensional new state
from
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})

to
a state

(\boldsymbol{x}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{v}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u})

of dimension
n+1

where
(\boldsymbol{y},\boldsymbol{u})

is the result of

s^{({n+1})}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
.

By
(H5)
,
the first
n
components of the new

n+1
-dimensional state

{\Phi^{(n+1)}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u}_{0})

is

\mathsf{take}_{{n}}({\Phi^{(n+1)}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))={\Phi^{(n)}}(\mathsf{take}_{{n}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))={\Phi^{(n)}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})=(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})

and by the definition of slice
the
(n+1)
-th component of the new state is

\mathsf{drop}_{{n}}({\Phi^{(n+1)}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}))=s^{({n+1})}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}).

Hence the new state computed by
Step 4.ii and Step 4.ii’ are the same.

The slice function
s^{({n})}
is useful when
the involution is computationally expensive
but has a light slice function.
After Step 4.ii is replaced by Step 4.ii’,
the Hyrbid NP-iMCMC sampler
need only to run the involution once
(
Item

3
)
and any subsequent “re-runs” (
Item

4
)
can be performed by the slice function.

If the slice function
s^{({n})}
is
implemented as
slice
[
n
]
in SPCF,
Line 11 in
NPiMCMC

can be changed from

(
x
,
v
)

=

involution
[
n
](
x0
,
v0
)

to

⬇

(
x
’,
v
’)

=

slice
[
n
](
x0
,
v0
);

(
x
,
v
)

=

(
x

+

x
’,

v

+

v
’)

D.2.1
Example (HMC)

Momentum update is the most computationally
heavy component in the HMC sampler.
Hence it would be useful if it has a
lightweight slice function.

In the setting of Hyrbid NP-iMCMC,
we assume the trace space

\mathbb{T}
is a list measurable space of
the Real measurable space
\mathbb{R}
.
Then, the
n
-dimensional momentum update

\phi^{M}_{k}
is an endofunction on

\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined as

\phi^{M}_{k}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}):=(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}-k{\nabla U}(\boldsymbol{q}))

where

U(\boldsymbol{q}):=-\log\ \max\{w(\boldsymbol{t})\mid\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{q})\}.

is the
n
-dimensional potential energy.

Given a
n
-dimensional state

(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p})
where

\boldsymbol{t}\in\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{q})\cap\text{Supp}(w)

has dimension lower than
n
,
the gradient of the potential energy
U
at
\boldsymbol{q}

w.r.t. the
n
-th coordinate is zero.
Hence,

(\mathsf{drop}_{{n-1}}\circ\phi^{M}_{k})(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p})=\mathsf{drop}_{{n-1}}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}-k{\nabla U}(\boldsymbol{q}))=({\boldsymbol{q}}^{n},{\boldsymbol{p}}^{n}),

and the slice of the momentum update

\phi^{M}_{k}

is simply the projection

\mathsf{drop}_{{n-1}}(\boldsymbol{q},\boldsymbol{p}):=({\boldsymbol{q}}^{n},{\boldsymbol{p}}^{n}).

However, not
all

2L

momentum updates in the re-runs of
the leapfrog function
\boldsymbol{L}
can be replaced
by its slice
\mathsf{drop}_{{n-1}}
.
This is because
when the dimension increments to say
n+1
,
only the extended initial state

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u}_{0})

has the property that it has an instance
with dimension lower than
n+1

and not the intermediate states.

D.3
Multiple Step NP-iMCMC

Say the involution of a Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler
is comprised of a list of
bijective endofunctions on
\mathbb{S}^{(n)}
,
namely
{\Phi^{(n)}}:={f^{({n})}_{{L}}}\circ\dots\circ{f^{({n})}_{{2}}}\circ{f^{({n})}_{{1}}}
.
To compute the new state,
we can either

•

apply the involution
{\Phi^{(n)}}

to the initial state

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})

in one go and
check whether the result
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})

has an instance in the
support of
w
, or

•

for each
\ell=1,\dots,L
,
apply the endofunction
{f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}

to

(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell-1},\boldsymbol{v}_{\ell-1})

and (immediately)
check whether the intermediate state

(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{v}_{\ell})
has
an instance in the support of
w
.

The Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler presented in
Section

B.3

takes the first option as
it is conceptually simpler.
However,
the second option is just as valid
and more importantly
give us the requirements needed to
replace each endofunction
by its slice in any subsequent “re-runs”.

D.3.1
The Multiple Step NP-iMCMC Algorithm

Assume the target density
w

satisfies
(V1)
and
(V3)
; and
for each
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
,
there is a probability kernel
K^{({n})}
and
a list of
L
bijective endofunctions

\{{f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}:\mathbb{S}^{(n)}\to\mathbb{S}^{(n)}\mid\ell=1,\dots,L\}_{n}

such that for each
\ell
,

\{{f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}\}_{n}
satisfies the
projection commutation property
(
(V5)
)
and for each
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
,
their composition

{f^{({n})}_{{L}}}\circ\dots\circ{f^{({n})}_{{1}}}

is involutive.

Let
s^{({n})}_{\ell}
be a slice of
the endofunction
{f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}
.
Given a SPCF program
M
with
weight function
w
on the trace space,
the
Multiple Step NP-iMCMC
sampler
generates a Markov chain as follows.
Given a current sample
\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
of dimension
k_{0}
,

1.

(Initialisation Step)
Form a
{k_{0}}
-dimensional parameter variable

\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({k_{0}})}}
by
pairing each value

{\boldsymbol{t}_{0}}^{i}
in

\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
with
a randomly drawn value
t

of the other type to make
a pair

({\boldsymbol{t}_{0}}^{i},t)
or
(t,{\boldsymbol{t}_{0}}^{i})

in the entropy space
{\mathbb{E}}
.

2.

(Stochastic Step)
Introduce randomness to the sampler by
drawing a
{k_{0}}
-dimensional value

\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({k_{0}})}}

from the probability measure

K^{({{k_{0}}})}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\cdot)
.

3.

(Multiple Step)
Initialise
\ell=1
.
If
\ell=L
,
proceed to Step 4
with
\boldsymbol{t}
as the proposed sample;
otherwise

3.1.

(Deterministic Step)
Compute the
\ell
-th state

(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{v}_{\ell})
by applying
the endofunction
{f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}
to

(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell-1},\boldsymbol{v}_{\ell-1})
where

n=\dim{(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell-1})}
.

3.2.

(Extend Step)
Test whether any instance
\boldsymbol{t}
of

\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}
is
in the support of
w
.
If so,
go to Step 3 with an incremented
\ell
;
otherwise
(none of the instances of
\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}
is
in the support of
w
),

3.2.i

Extend and replace
the
n
-dimensional initial state
from
(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})

to a state
(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u}_{0})

of dimension
n+1

where
\boldsymbol{y}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
are values
drawn randomly
from
\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(n+1)}-\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}{(n)}}}
and

\mu_{{\mathbb{E}}^{\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}{(n+1)}-\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}{(n)}}}

respectively.

3.2.ii

For each
i=1,\dots,\ell
,
extend and replace
the
n
-dimensional
i
-th
intermediate state from

(\boldsymbol{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{v}_{i})

to a state
(\boldsymbol{x}_{i}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{y}_{i},\boldsymbol{v}_{i}\mathbin{+\mkern-8.0mu+}\boldsymbol{u}_{i})

of dimension
n+1

where
(\boldsymbol{y}_{i},\boldsymbol{u}_{i})

is the result of

s^{({n+1})}_{i}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i-1},\boldsymbol{v}_{i-1})
.

3.2.iii

Go to Step 3.2 with the
extended
n+1
-dimensional states
(\boldsymbol{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{v}_{i})

for
i=0,\dots,\ell
.

4.

(Accept/reject Step)
Accept the proposed sample
\boldsymbol{t}

as the next sample with probability

\displaystyle\min\bigg\{1;\;

\displaystyle\frac{w{(\boldsymbol{t})}\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k})}}(\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{L},\boldsymbol{v}_{L}))\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{L})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{L})}{w{(\boldsymbol{t}_{0})}\cdot{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({k_{0}})}}(\mathsf{take}_{{k_{0}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}))\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\cdot\varphi_{{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{0})}\cdot\prod_{\ell=1}^{L}|{\det({\nabla{f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell-1},\boldsymbol{v}_{\ell-1})})}|\bigg\}

where
n=\dim{(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})}=\dim{(\boldsymbol{v}_{0})}
,

k
is the dimension of
\boldsymbol{t}
and

k_{0}
is the dimension of
{\boldsymbol{t}_{0}}
;
otherwise reject the proposal and repeat
\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
.

Unlike in Hybrid NP-iMCMC,
the Multiple Step NP-iMCMC sampler computes
the intermediate states

\{(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{v}_{\ell})\}_{\ell=1,\dots,L}

one-by-one, making sure in Step 3.2 that
each of these state
(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{v}_{\ell})

has an instance in the support of
w
.
Hence when the dimension is incremented
from
n
to
n+1

we can use the slice functions
to extend intermediate states to states of
dimension
n+1
.

Remark D.1
.

The Multiple Step NP-iMCMC sampler can be
seen as
a generalisation of Hybrid NP-iMCMC
(and hence a generalisation of NP-iMCMC (
Section

B.3.2
))
as we can recover
Hybrid NP-iMCMC by setting
L
to one and taking the
involution
{\Phi^{(n)}}
as the only endofunction in Multiple Step NP-iMCMC.

D.3.2

D.3.3
Correctness of Multiple Step NP-iMCMC Algorithm

The Multiple Step NP-iMCMC sampler cannot be formulated
as an instance of Hybrid NP-iMCMC
and requires a separate proof.
Nonetheless, the arguments are similar.

•

Proposition

B.3
tells us that
as long as
w
almost surely
terminates (
(V3)
),
the measure of a
n
-dimensional
parameter variable not having any instances
in the support of
w
tends to zero
as the dimension
n
tends to infinity.
As
{f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}
is bijective
(and hence invertible),
the Multiple Step NP-iMCMC sampler
almost surely satisfies
the condition in the loop
in Step 3.2
and hence almost surely terminates.

•

Next, we identify the state distribution
of Multiple Step NP-iMCMC.
We say a
n
-dimensional state

(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
is
valid
if

(i)

For all
\ell=1,\dots,L
,

\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell})\cap\text{Supp}(w)\not=\varnothing

where

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}):=(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
and

(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{v}_{\ell}):={f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell-1},\boldsymbol{v}_{\ell-1})
; and

(ii)

For all
\ell=1,\dots,L
,

\mathsf{instance}(\boldsymbol{y}_{\ell})\cap\text{Supp}(w)\not=\varnothing

where

(\boldsymbol{y}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}_{0}):=(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
and

(\boldsymbol{y}_{L-\ell+1},\boldsymbol{u}_{L-\ell+1}):={{{f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}}^{-1}}(\boldsymbol{y}_{L-\ell},\boldsymbol{u}_{L-\ell})
; and

(iii)

For all
k<n
,

\mathsf{take}_{{k}}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
is not a valid state.

Then, we can define the state distribution
and show that the state movement in Multiple
Step NP-iMCMC is invariant against this distribution.

•

Finally,
we conclude by

Lemma

B.17
that
the Multiple Step NP-iMCMC sampler is correct.

D.3.4
Transforming Multiple Step NP-iMCMC Sampler

Recall we discussed three techniques in

Appendix

C
,
each when applied to the Hybrid NP-iMCMC sampler,
improve its flexibility and/or efficiency.
We now see how these techniques can be applied
to Multiple Step NP-iMCMC.

This technique allows us to ‘mix’
Multiple Step NP-iMCMC samplers
in such a way that the resulting sampler still
preserves the posterior.
Given a collection of
Multiple Step NP-iMCMC samplers,
indexed by
m\in{{\mathbb{E}}^{\alpha}}
for some
\alpha\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
,
the
State-dependent Multiple Step NP-iMCMC Mixture
sampler
draws an indicator
m\in{{\mathbb{E}}^{\alpha}}

from a probability measure

K_{M}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\cdot)
on
{{\mathbb{E}}^{\alpha}}

where
K_{M}:\bigcup_{n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\leadsto{{\mathbb{E}}^{\alpha}}

is a probability kernel and

\boldsymbol{x}_{0}
is the parameter variable
constructed from
the current sample
\boldsymbol{t}_{0}
in

Item

1
.
A proposal
\boldsymbol{t}
is then generated by running
Steps 2 and 3 of
the
m
-indexed
Multiple Step NP-iMCMC sampler,
and is accepted with
a modified probability that
includes the probability of picking
m
.

Pseudocode

gives the
SPCF implementation of this sampler as
the
MixtureMSNPiMCMC
function.
(Terms specific to this technique
are highlighted.)
We assume the SPCF term

mixkernel

implements the mixture kernel
K_{M}
;

pdfmixkernel
implements
the probability density function

{\mathsf{pdf}K_{M}}
; and
for each
m\in{{\mathbb{E}}^{\alpha}}
and
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
,

auxkernel
[
n
][
m
]

implements the auxiliary kernel and

pdfauxkernel
[
n
][
m
]

implements its density;

f
[
n
][
l
][
m
]

implements the endofunction

slice
[
n
][
l
][
m
]

implements its slice
and

absdetjacf
[
n
][
l
][
m
]
implements
the absolute value of the
Jacobian determinant of
the endofunction
of the
m
-indexed
Multiple Step NP-iMCMC sampler.

Correctness

MixtureMSNPiMCMC
can be formulated as
an instance of
MultistepNPiMCMC

with
auxiliary kernel
mixauxkernel
[
n
]

and its density
mixpdfauxkernel
[
n
]
and

L
number of endofunctions

mixf
[
n
][
l
]

(
l
ranges from
1
to
L
)
for each dimension
n
with slice

mixslice
[
n
][
l
]
and the absolute value of
its Jacobian determinant

absdetjacmixf
[
n
][
l
]
;
parameter and auxiliary index maps

mixindexX
and
mixindexY
and
projection
mixproj

given in

.

This technique allows us to relax
the assumption that the composition

{f^{({n})}_{{L}}}\circ\dots\circ{f^{({n})}_{{2}}}\circ{f^{({n})}_{{1}}}

is involutive.
Assume for
\ell=1,\dots,L
, both sets

\{{f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}\}_{n}
and

\{{{{f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}}^{-1}}\}_{n}

satisfy the projection commutation property
(
(V5)
),
the
Direction Multiple Step NP-iMCMC

sampler randomly employ either

{f^{({n})}_{{L}}}\circ\dots\circ{f^{({n})}_{{2}}}\circ{f^{({n})}_{{1}}}

or

{{{f^{({n})}_{{1}}}}^{-1}}\circ{{{f^{({n})}_{{2}}}}^{-1}}\circ\dots\circ{{{f^{({n})}_{{L}}}}^{-1}}

to move around the
n
-dimensional state space
and proposes a new sample.

Pseudocode

gives the
SPCF implementation of this sampler as

DirectionMSNPiMCMC
function.
(Terms specific to this technique are highlighted.)
We assume for each
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
and

d\in\mathbb{2}
,
the SPCF term

f
[
n
][
l
][
True
]
implements
the endofunction
{f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}
and

f
[
n
][
l
][
False
]
implements
the inverse
{{{f^{({n})}_{{L-\ell+1}}}}^{-1}}
;

slice
[
n
][
l
][
True
]
implements
the slice of
{f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}
and

slice
[
n
][
l
][
False
]
implements
the slice of
{{{f^{({n})}_{{L-\ell+1}}}}^{-1}}
; and

absdetjacf
[
n
][
l
][
True
]
implements
the absolute value of the Jacobian determinant of
{f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}
and

absdetjacf
[
n
][
l
][
False
]
implements
that of
{f^{({n})}_{{L-\ell+1}}}
.

Correctness

DirectionMSNPiMCMC
can be formulated as an instance of

MultistepNPiMCMC

with
auxiliary kernel
dirauxkernel
[
n
]

and its density
pdfdirauxkernel
[
n
]
and

dirL
number of endofunctions

dirf
[
n
][
l
]

(
l
ranges from
1
to
dirL
)
for each dimension
n
with slice

dirslice
[
n
][
l
]
and the absolute value of
its Jacobian determinant

absdetjacf
[
n
][
l
]
;
parameter and auxiliary index maps

dirindexX
and
dirindexY
and
projection
dirproj

given in

.
Note the
dirf
[
n
]
function denotes the
composition that
flips the direction after applying
the endofunctions
{f^{({n})}_{{\ell}}}
for

\ell=1,\dots,L

with an inverse
the flips the direction and then
apply the endofunctions
{f^{({n})}_{{L-\ell+1}}}
for

\ell=1,\dots,L
.

This technique gives us a method to
construct irreversible Multiple Step NP-iMCMC samplers.
The key is to persist the direction
from a previous iteration.

The
Persistent Multiple Step NP-iMCMC

sampler keeps trace of a direction variable

d_{0}\in\mathbb{2}

(instead of sampling a fresh one at the start)
and use it to determine
the auxiliary kernel
(
K^{({n})}_{\mathsf{T}}:{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\leadsto{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}

or

K^{({n})}_{\mathsf{F}}:{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\leadsto{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}
)
and list of endofunctions
(
{f^{({n})}_{{L}}}\circ\dots\circ{f^{({n})}_{{1}}}
or

{{{f^{({n})}_{{1}}}}^{-1}}\circ\dots\circ{{{f^{({n})}_{{L}}}}^{-1}}
)
employed.
This direction variable is flipped
strategically to
make the resulting algorithm irreversible.

Pseudocode

gives
the SPCF implementation of this sampler as
the function

PersistentMSNPiMCMC
.
(Terms specific to this technique are highlighted.)
In addition to the SPCF terms in
DirectionMSNPiMCMC
,
the SPCF term

auxkernel
[
n
][
True
]
implements
the auxiliary kernel
K^{({n})}_{\mathsf{T}}
and

pdfauxkernel
[
n
][
True
]

implements its density
{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({n})}}_{\mathsf{T}}

and

auxkernel
[
n
][
False
]
implements
the auxiliary kernel
K^{({n})}_{\mathsf{F}}
and

pdfauxkernel
[
n
][
False
]

implements its density
{\mathsf{pdf}K^{({n})}}_{\mathsf{F}}
.
Note that

PersistentMSNPiMCMC

updates samples
on the space
{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}\times\mathbb{2}
,
which can easily be marginalised to
{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}

by taking the first
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n)
components.

Correctness

Consider the
MultistepNPiMCMC
function
with
auxiliary kernel
perauxkernel
[
n
]

and its density
pdfperauxkernel
[
n
]
and

perL
number of endofunctions

perf
[
n
][
l
]

(
l
ranges from
1
to
perL
)
for each dimension
n
with slice

perslice
[
n
][
l
]
and the absolute value of
its Jacobian determinant

absdetjacperf
[
n
][
l
]
;
parameter and auxiliary index maps

perindexX
and
perindexY
and
projection
perproj

given in

.

The
MultistepNPiMCMC
function with
the primitives indicated in

is
almost
equivalent to

PersistentMSNPiMCMC
,
except

MultistepNPiMCMC

induces a transition kernel on
{\mathbb{E}}\times{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}

whereas
PersistentMSNPiMCMC

induces a transition kernel on
\mathbb{2}\times{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}
; and
when the proposal
t
is accepted,

MultistepNPiMCMC

returns
d
whereas

PersistentMSNPiMCMC
returns

not

d
.

By composing
MultistepNPiMCMC

with
flipdir
which flips the direction and
marginalising the Markov chain generated
by the composition from

{\mathbb{E}}\times{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}
to

\mathbb{2}\times{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}
,
we get

PersistentMSNPiMCMC
.

Appendix E
Examples of Nonparametric Involutive MCMC

In this section, we design
novel
nonparametric samplers using the
Hybrid NP-iMCMC method described in
Appendix

B

or the Multiple Step NP-iMCMC method
described in
Appendix

D
.

We assume the target density function

w
on the
trace space
\mathbb{T}
is tree representable
and satisfies
(H1)
and
(H3)
.
Specifications of the auxiliary kernels and involutions are given for each sampler.

E.1

As discussed in
Section

,
the standard MH sampler can be seen as an instance of
the iMCMC sampler
with the proposal distribution

as the auxiliary kernel and
a swap function as the involution.

Suppose
a proposal kernel

for all

),
the Hybrid NP-iMCMC method
(

)
gives an nonparametric extension of the MH sampler.

E.2
Nonparametric Metropolis-Hastings with Persistence

Following the persistent technique described in
Section

C.3
for Hybrid NP-iMCMC,
we can form a nonreversible variant of
the NP-MH sampler described in
Section

E.1
.
We call the resulting algorithm
the Nonparametric Metropolis-Hastings with Persistence (NP-MH-P) sampler.

Suppose
a proposal kernel
q^{(n)}:{\mathbb{E}}^{n}\leadsto{\mathbb{E}}^{n}
exists
for each dimension
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
.
Similar to NP-MH,
both
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}
and
\iota_{{\mathbb{Y}}}
are
set to be identities
(which means

{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}={{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}={\mathbb{E}}^{n}

for all
n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}
).
Following
(
Turitsyn et al. 2011
)
,
given a parameter variable
\boldsymbol{x}\in{\mathbb{E}}^{n}
,
we can partition the auxiliary space
{\mathbb{E}}^{n}
into two sets

U_{\boldsymbol{x},+}:=\{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{E}}^{n}\mid\eta(\boldsymbol{v})\geq\eta(\boldsymbol{x})\}
and

U_{\boldsymbol{x},-}:=\{\boldsymbol{v}\in{\mathbb{E}}^{n}\mid\eta(\boldsymbol{v})<\boldsymbol{x}(\boldsymbol{x})\}

where

\eta:{\mathbb{E}}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is some measurable function;
and form two kernels

{K^{(n)}}_{+}
and
{K^{(n)}}_{-}
from
q^{(n)}
defined as

{K^{(n)}}_{+}(\boldsymbol{x},V):=\frac{q^{(n)}(V\cap U_{\boldsymbol{x},+})}{q^{(n)}(U_{\boldsymbol{x},+})}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad{K^{(n)}}_{-}(\boldsymbol{x},V):=\frac{q^{(n)}(V\cap U_{\boldsymbol{x},-})}{q^{(n)}(U_{\boldsymbol{x},-})}.

(3)

Using the Persistent (Hybrid) NP-iMCMC sampler as described in
Section

C.3
,
a nonreversible variant of NP-MH can be
formed.

The Nonparametric Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (NP-HMC)
is a MCMC sampler
introduced by
(
Mak et al. 2021b
)

for probabilistic programming.
Here we show that it is
an instance of the
Direction Multiple Step NP-iMCMC sampler
(
Section

D.3.6
).

Typically, the Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) sampler takes
a target density on
\mathbb{R}^{n}

and proposes
a new state by
simulating
L
leapfrog steps:

\boldsymbol{L}:=(\phi^{M}_{\epsilon/2}\circ\phi^{P}_{\epsilon}\circ\phi^{M}_{\epsilon/2})^{L}

where

\phi^{M}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}):=(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}-\epsilon{\nabla U}(\boldsymbol{x}))
and

\phi^{P}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}):=(\boldsymbol{x}+\epsilon\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{v})

are the momentum and position updates
with step size
\epsilon
respectively.
Notice that that the momentum and position updates
satisfy projection commutation property (
(H5)
),
have inverses

{{(\phi^{M}_{\epsilon})}^{-1}}=M\circ\phi^{M}_{\epsilon}\circ M
and

{{(\phi^{P}_{\epsilon})}^{-1}}=M\circ\phi^{P}_{\epsilon}\circ M

where
M(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}):=(\boldsymbol{x},-\boldsymbol{v})

and slices

\mathsf{drop}_{{n-1}}
(for
\phi^{M}_{\epsilon/2}
,
see
Section

D.2.1
for more details) and

(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\mapsto({\boldsymbol{x}}^{n}+\epsilon{\boldsymbol{v}}^{n},{\boldsymbol{v}}^{n})

(for
\phi^{P}_{\epsilon}
)
respectively.
Moreover,
the absolute value of the Jacobian determinant
of both updates are

|{\det{{\nabla\phi^{M}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})}}}|=|{\det{{\nabla\phi^{P}_{\epsilon/2}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})}}}|=1
.

Given a target density
w
on

\bigcup_{n\in\mathsf{\mathbb{N}}}\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the HMC sampler can be extended using the
Direction Multiple Step NP-iMCMC sampler.
Given an input sample
\boldsymbol{t}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{0}}
,
a
k_{0}
-dimensional initial state

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})
is formed
where
\boldsymbol{x}_{0}:=\boldsymbol{t}_{0}

and
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
drawn from

K^{({n})}(\boldsymbol{x},\cdot):=\mathcal{N}_{n}
.
A direction variable
d_{0}
is drawn to
determine
whether the leapfrog steps
\boldsymbol{L}

or its inverse
{{\boldsymbol{L}}^{-1}}

is performed on the initial state

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})
,
one update at a time,
extending the dimension as required.
Say the initial state is extended to a

n
-dimensional state

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})
and
is traversed to the
n
-dimensional new state

(\boldsymbol{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{v}^{*})

which has an instance
\boldsymbol{t}

in the support of
w
.

\boldsymbol{t}
is returned with probability

\displaystyle\min\bigg\{1;\;

\displaystyle\frac{w{(\boldsymbol{t})}\cdot\varphi_{n}(\boldsymbol{x}^{*})\cdot\varphi_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}^{*})}{w{(\boldsymbol{t}_{0})}\cdot\varphi_{n}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0})\cdot\varphi_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}_{0})}\bigg\}.

Pseudocode of NP-HMC

gives the
SPCF implementations

leapfrog
[
n
][
m
]

and
leapfrogslice
[
n
][
m
]
,
where

leapfrog
[
n
][
m
][
True
]

and
leapfrogslice
[
n
][
m
][
True
]

return the
m
-th endofunction
and its slice in
the composition

(\phi^{M}_{\epsilon/2}\circ\phi^{P}_{\epsilon}\circ\phi^{M}_{\epsilon/2})^{L}

of
3L
updates respectively;
and similarly,

leapfrog
[
n
][
m
][
False
]

and
leapfrogslice
[
n
][
m
][
False
]

return the
m
-th endofunction
and its slice in

({{\phi^{M}_{\epsilon/2}}^{-1}}\circ{{\phi^{P}_{\epsilon}}^{-1}}\circ{{\phi^{M}_{\epsilon/2}}^{-1}})^{L}.

gives the SPCF implementation
NPHMC

of the NP-HMC sampler
as an instance of the Direction
Multiple Step NP-iMCMC sampler.
Importantly, the expensive

leapfrog
[
n
][
m
]
function is called once
for each
m
ranging from
1
to
3
L

the lightweight
leapfrogslice

is called in any subsequent re-runs.

Correctness

Since both

\phi^{M}_{\epsilon/2}
and

\phi^{P}_{\epsilon}

are bijective and satisfies the projection commutation property
(
(H5)
),
the correctness of NP-HMC is implied by
the correctness of Direction Multiple Step
NP-iMCMC sampler.

With the catalogue of techniques
explored in
Section

D.3.4
,
novel irreversible variants of
the NP-HMC algorithm can be formed.
Here we focus on
the NP-HMC with Persistence algorithm
which can be seen as a nonparametric extension
of the Generalised HMC algorithm

(
Horowitz 1991
)
.

E.4.1
Generalised HMC

Horowitz 1991
made two changes to
the conventional HMC algorithm in order to
generate an irreversible Markov chain
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

and improve its performance.

1.

A “corrupted” momentum is
used to move round the state space.

2.

The direction is “persisted”
if the proposal is accepted;
otherwise it is negated.

The resulting sampler is called the

Generalised HMC
algorithm as it is a
generalisation of the typical HMC sampler.

As shown in
(
Neklyudov et al. 2020
)
,
the Generalised HMC algorithm can be
presented as a composition of
an iMCMC algorithm that
“corrupts” the momentum and
a Persistent iMCMC algorithm
that uses Hamiltonian dynamics to find a new state
with a persisting direction.
We consider a similar approach in
our construction of a nonparametric extension
of Generalised HMC.

E.4.2
NP-HMC with Persistence

State Density

Let the state
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
has density

w^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v}):=w(\boldsymbol{x})

w.r.t. the normal distribution
\mathcal{N}_{2n}
.
It is clear that this density

w^{\prime}
is integrable (
(H1)
)
and almost surely terminating (
(H3)
).
By setting the parameter index map
to
\iota_{{\mathbb{X}}}(n):=2n

and parameter space
{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}:=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
the state
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})
of length
2n

is a
n
-dimensional parameter variable.

HMCw
in

is a SPCF implementation of
w^{\prime}
.

Corrupt Momentum

Given the current state
(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

with direction
d_{0}\in\mathbb{2}
,
a new momentum is drawn from the
distribution

{\mathcal{N}_{n}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}},\alpha^{2})

for a hyper-parameter
\alpha\in[0,1)
.

This can be presented in the NP-iMCMC format
with the auxiliary variable
\boldsymbol{u}
sampled from

{\mathcal{N}_{n}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}},\alpha^{2})

and the swap

(((\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}),d_{0}),\boldsymbol{u})\mapsto(((\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{u}),d_{0}),\boldsymbol{v}_{0})

as the involution.
Since the new state
(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{u})

always have an instance in
the support of
w^{\prime}
,
and the acceptance ratio is

\displaystyle\min\Big\{1,\frac{w^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\varphi_{2n}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{u})\cdot\mathsf{pdf}_{\mathbb{2}}(d_{0})\cdot\varphi_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\mid\boldsymbol{u}\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}},\alpha^{2})}{w^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\cdot\varphi_{2n}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\cdot\mathsf{pdf}_{\mathbb{2}}(d_{0})\cdot\varphi_{n}(\boldsymbol{u}\mid\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\sqrt{1-\alpha^{2}},\alpha^{2})}\Big\}=1,

the extend step (
Item

3
)
and
the accept/reject step (
Item

4
)
of the NP-iMCMC sampler
can both be skipped.
This results in a sampler that has the
SPCF implementation

CorruptMom
in
.

Persist Momentum

We consider the Persistent Multiple Step NP-iMCMC
algorithm (
Section

D.3.7
)
with the target density

w^{\prime}
as follows.

Given a
k_{0}
-dimensional
parameter

(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})\in{{\mathbb{X}}^{({n})}}:=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
direction
d_{0}\in\mathbb{2}
,
a
dummy
auxiliary variable
\boldsymbol{u}\in{{\mathbb{Y}}^{({n})}}:=\mathbb{R}^{n}

is sampled from

K^{({n})}((\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}),\cdot):=\mathcal{N}_{n}

to form an initial state

((\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}),\boldsymbol{u})
.
Depending on the direction
d_{0}
,
either

\big((\phi^{M}_{\epsilon/2}\times\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})\circ(\phi^{P}_{\epsilon}\times\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})\circ(\phi^{M}_{\epsilon/2}\times\mathsf{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})\big)^{L}

or its inverse
is performed on

((\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}),\boldsymbol{u})
,
one update at a time,
extending the dimension as required.
Say the initial state is extended to a

n
-dimensional

((\boldsymbol{x}_{0}^{*},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}^{*}),\boldsymbol{u}^{*})
and
is traversed to the
n
-dimensional new state

((\boldsymbol{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{v}^{*}),\boldsymbol{u}^{*})
.
Then, the instance

(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})\in\text{Supp}(w^{\prime})
of
the
n
-dimensional parameter

(\boldsymbol{x}^{*},\boldsymbol{v}^{*})

is returned
alongside the direction variable
d_{0}

with probability

\displaystyle\min\bigg\{1;\;

\displaystyle\frac{w^{\prime}{(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{v})}\cdot\varphi_{n}(\boldsymbol{x}^{*})\cdot\varphi_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}^{*})}{w^{\prime}{(\boldsymbol{x}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0})}\cdot\varphi_{n}(\boldsymbol{x}_{0}^{*})\cdot\varphi_{n}(\boldsymbol{v}_{0}^{*})}\bigg\}.

Note that the auxiliary variable
\boldsymbol{u}

has no effect on the sampler.
Hence,

gives a SPCF implementation

PersistMom
where
the stochastic step (Step 2) is skipped.

NP-HMC with Persistence

Composing the samplers
which “corrupts”
and persists the momentum
gives us the
NP-HMC with Persistence

algorithm, which is an nonparametric
extension of Generalised HMC.

gives the SPCF
implementation
NPHMCwPersistent

by composing
CorruptMom

and
PersistMom
.

Last but not least, we extend the
Look Ahead HMC algorithm
(
Sohl-Dickstein et al. 2014
)
,
which is equivalent to
the Extra Chance Generalised HMC algorithm
(
